Upgrading Cacti Under UNIX Compatible Operating Systems


Use only the supported operating systems <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Requirements.md#supported-operating-systems> and maintained
dependencies compatible with this Cacti branch.


This procedure covers a source installation upgrading to Cacti development
(1.3). For a
package-managed installation, use the package maintainer's upgrade procedure
and configuration-file handling. See the separate guides for
Windows <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Upgrading-Cacti-Under-Windows.md> and
FreeBSD <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Upgrading-Cacti-Under-FreeBSD.md>.


Review the release notes <https://github.com/Cacti/cacti/releases> and requirements <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Requirements.md> first.
Schedule time to back up and restore the installation. Test the upgrade on an
isolated copy of the database and files before changing production. Keep
dependency upgrades separate unless a prerequisite requires them.


Stop writers before backing up


Turn off Data Collection Enabled under the poller settings. Also suspend
the actual scheduler on each affected collector: disable only the Cacti cron
entry, or stop its installed cactid service. Preserve the scheduler settings
so they can be restored later. Do not disable all system cron jobs.


Wait for existing poller, Spine, script-server, Boost and other background jobs
to finish. Prevent web requests and plugin jobs from changing the installation
during the backup and upgrade. The application polling switch alone does not
stop every running process or administrative writer.


If collectors use rrdcached, drain its pending writes using the procedure for
that deployment before taking the RRD snapshot. Keep writers stopped until the
database and file backups are both complete. Coordinate this on remote
collectors as well as the main server.


Back up the database and complete installation


Record the installed Cacti, Spine, PHP and database versions, installation
paths, service accounts, scheduler entries and remote-collector configuration.
Store backups outside the web root with access restricted to administrators.


Back up the Cacti database with a client compatible with the database server.
For example, with the application stopped and a backup account with the
required privileges:


umask 077
BACKUP_DIR=/srv/backups/cacti-before-1.3-dev
mkdir "$BACKUP_DIR"
mysqldump --user=backup_user --password --lock-tables --quick \
  --routines --events --triggers cacti \
  > "$BACKUP_DIR/cacti.sql"



Adjust the database name, account and paths before running these examples. Use
mariadb-dump instead of mysqldump where appropriate. Enter the password at
the prompt. Stop if a command fails; an output file alone does not prove a
successful dump. The account needs the privileges required by the installed
dump client, including table locks for the example above.


--single-transaction provides a consistent snapshot for transactional
tables; it does not make nontransactional tables consistent. Inspect the
actual storage engines and choose the backup method accordingly. Keep all
application writers stopped for the corresponding file snapshot. See the
database backup documentation <https://dev.mysql.com/doc/refman/8.0/en/mysqldump.html> for the option and privilege details.


Back up the complete installation, including the RRD files. For an example
installation at /var/www/html/cacti:


tar -czpf "$BACKUP_DIR/cacti-files.tar.gz" -C /var/www/html cacti
tar -tzf "$BACKUP_DIR/cacti-files.tar.gz"



Preserve include/config.php, plugins, custom scripts, query resources and
other local configuration. Include CSRF secret material, including an alternate
$path_csrf_secret from the configuration. Back up separately any RRD,
configuration or resource paths outside the installation tree. A tar archive
normally preserves a symlink rather than including its external target. Retain
the permissions and ownership needed for restoration.


Save the database user/grant definitions and any connection certificates needed
to recreate access. These are not included in a dump of the Cacti database
alone. Include site or plugin SQL routines and events, as in the example, and
account for their additional backup privileges.


Back up remote-collector databases and local files where they hold state.
Verify dump completion and archive contents, then rehearse restoration on an
isolated system. Excluding *.rrd would leave monitoring history outside the
recovery set.


Check development dependencies


Development source requires PHP 8.3 or later in the PHP 8 series, with the
extensions declared by the selected revision. Install locked dependencies in
the staging directory with composer install --no-dev, then run composer check-platform-reqs --no-dev. Preserve this complete dependency tree when
deploying the staged application. Do not run composer update against a live
installation. A complete release bundle can already include dependencies;
verify its platform checks instead of assuming that every archive is complete.


Install the staged files


Obtain the intended development revision and stage it outside the live
installation. Record its commit and verify its provenance. If using a release
archive instead, follow that release's requirements and verification steps.
Keep the existing installation and its backup until the upgrade has been
checked.


Copy the contents of the prepared staging directory into the existing
installation, not the directory itself. For example:


STAGING_DIR=/var/tmp/cacti-1.3-dev
CACTI_DIR=/var/www/html/cacti
cp -a "$STAGING_DIR"/. "$CACTI_DIR"/



Confirm that the new files are directly under the installation root, such as
$CACTI_DIR/poller.php, rather than a nested cacti-1.3-dev directory.
Preserve the existing include/config.php and review new options in
include/config.php.dist. Reconcile local code changes and plugin
compatibility with the new files; copying the release is not a substitute for
reviewing those changes.


Check permissions using the accounts that actually run the web server, poller
and maintenance jobs. Those accounts need not be identical. Give the poller the
required access to RRD and log paths, and check configured cache, resource and
script paths. Preserve access needed by installation and package imports. Do
not make the entire application tree writable as a permissions workaround.


Run and verify the upgrade


Allow an administrator to open the Cacti URL and follow the database-upgrade
workflow. Review errors before proceeding. The release also provides
cli/upgrade_database.php; consult its --help output if using the CLI
workflow. Do not manually advance the database version or force a starting
version merely to bypass an upgrade check.


Confirm the installed version, database-upgrade result, credentials, paths,
plugin state and collector configuration. Check remote collectors individually
and follow the applicable synchronization procedure; do not assume that two
polling cycles prove their files and databases have upgraded successfully.


Restore web access and the saved scheduler configuration only after the upgrade
checks succeed, then re-enable collection. Use either the configured cron path
or the daemon for a collector, not both. Verify logs, data freshness and
representative RRD updates after normal polling resumes.


Recovery


If the upgrade fails, keep writers stopped while deciding whether to retry or
restore. Replacing PHP files alone does not undo a database migration. Restore
the matching pre-upgrade database, application files, RRD data and external
configuration or secret material, including affected remote-collector state.
Verify the restored installation before re-enabling its schedulers. Restoring
an earlier backup discards changes collected after that backup.




Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


Upgrading Cacti under Windows


Use only the supported operating systems <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Requirements.md#supported-operating-systems> and maintained
dependencies compatible with this Cacti branch.


These instructions cover upgrading a source/archive installation to Cacti
1.3-dev. For a managed Windows installation, also follow its package or
installer upgrade procedure; do not overwrite package-managed files with an
unrelated archive. Review the release
notes <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Version-Specific-Release-Notes.md> and requirements <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Requirements.md>
for the target Cacti version first. An upgrade of Cacti does not automatically
require upgrading Windows, PHP or the database.


The current source manifest requires ext-pcntl and ext-posix, which native
Windows PHP cannot provide. Read the Windows installation
prerequisite <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Installing-Under-Windows.md#development-packaging-prerequisite>
before scheduling downtime. Stop if the selected artifact rejects Windows.


Check development dependencies


Development source requires PHP 8.3 or later in the PHP 8 series, with the
extensions declared by the selected revision. Install locked dependencies in
the staging directory with composer install --no-dev, then run composer check-platform-reqs --no-dev. Preserve this complete dependency tree when
deploying the staged application. Do not run composer update against a live
installation. A complete release bundle can already include dependencies;
verify its platform checks instead of assuming that every archive is complete.


Pause collection and take a recoverable backup


* In Console > Configuration > Settings > Poller, clear Data Collection
Enabled and save. Disable the actual Windows Scheduled Task that launches
poller.php, or stop the service that manages your poller. Record the task
or service settings so you can restore them. Use Task Scheduler <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows-server/administration/windows-commands/schtasks-change> to
disable future starts; this alone does not finish an already running poll.


* Wait for running pollers, Spine processes, script servers and background
writers to finish. Include remote collectors and plugin jobs. Restrict web
access to the upgrade administrator so configuration changes cannot race the
backup. Flush pending RRD-cache writes before copying RRD files and confirm
that writers have stopped. Do not start a manual poll during this
maintenance window.


* Create a protected backup directory outside the web root. From Command
Prompt, use the dump utility matching your database server. Replace the
executable path, account, database name and output path in this example. Use
the actual database host, port and TLS options for your installation. The
account needs the backup privileges required by the installed server;
--password prompts rather than putting a password in the command.


"C:\path\to\mysqldump.exe" --user=YOUR_BACKUP_USER --password ^
  --lock-tables --quick --routines --events --triggers ^
  --databases cacti --result-file="D:\CactiBackup\cacti-YYYYMMDD.sql"



With all Cacti writers paused, this dump example <https://dev.mysql.com/doc/refman/8.0/en/mysqldump.html> uses table locks to
cover transactional and nontransactional tables in the Cacti database. A
transaction-only dump does not protect nontransactional tables. Use a new
output filename and verify successful completion: a failed dump may leave a
partial file. Do not proceed until the backup can be restored in a separate
test environment.


* Back up the existing Cacti tree, including hidden files, using a separate
destination outside the web root. For example, Robocopy <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows-server/administration/windows-commands/robocopy> can
preserve file permissions as well as contents when run with the required
privileges:


robocopy "C:\inetpub\wwwroot\cacti" "D:\CactiBackup\cacti-YYYYMMDD" /E /COPYALL /SL /XJ /R:2 /W:2



Inspect its report; exit codes of 8 or greater indicate copy failures.
Confirm that the backup includes include/config.php, RRDs, custom scripts,
query resources, plugins and secret files, including any alternate
path_csrf_secret. Also save configured paths outside this tree, the Spine
configuration, scheduler settings, web-server configuration, database grants
and required certificates/keys. Record symbolic links and junctions and back
up their external targets separately; the example copies symbolic links and
excludes junctions. Preserve permissions and record the installed versions.
Keep this file backup and database dump together as one recovery set.




Install and verify the upgrade


* Extract the target Cacti release <https://github.com/Cacti/cacti/releases> into a staging directory. Keep
the existing tree and verified backup available. Deploy the release files
using your existing installation layout, preserving the working
include/config.php and local resources. Compare the new
include/config.php.dist for changes; do not replace existing credentials,
collector IDs, paths or secrets with example defaults. Check plugin and
custom-script compatibility before restoring or enabling them.


* Verify file permissions for the web server and poller accounts. Open the
existing Cacti URL as an administrator and complete the database upgrade.
Review installer checks and logs. Use version-appropriate database settings
from Requirements <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Requirements.md>, rather than applying fixed global SQL
tuning commands.


* For remote collectors, keep their schedulers paused while updating their
local application files and configuration and completing the applicable
upgrade and synchronization. Verify each collector's reported version,
connectivity and logs; completion is not guaranteed after two poll cycles.
Check the matching Spine version where it is used.


* Re-enable Data Collection Enabled and restore the recorded scheduler or
service only after the upgrade succeeds. Check a complete poll cycle,
collector status, new RRD samples and representative graphs. If recovery is
necessary, stop writers again and restore the matching application files,
database, RRDs and configuration together; copying old PHP files over an
upgraded database is not a rollback.






Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


Upgrading Cacti Under FreeBSD


Use only the supported operating systems <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Requirements.md#supported-operating-systems> and maintained
dependencies compatible with this Cacti branch.


These instructions cover the FreeBSD upgrade procedure. This manual describes
Cacti development (1.3), while the FreeBSD port currently supplies Cacti 1.2.31.
Use the 1.2.x manual <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/tree/1.2.x> for release-specific requirements when
upgrading the packaged release. Determine whether Cacti is maintained by
pkg, by ports or from a source archive. Keep that installation
method for the upgrade; do not overlay an archive onto package-managed files.
Review the target Cacti release notes <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Version-Specific-Release-Notes.md>,
requirements <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Requirements.md> and package or port notes before proceeding. Do
not make an unrelated operating-system, PHP or database major-version upgrade
part of this procedure without a separate compatibility plan.


Pause collection and back up


* Clear Data Collection Enabled under `Console > Configuration > Settings





Poller`. Disable the Cacti-specific cron entry or stop its configured
poller service on every collector. Record what you change; do not stop the
system-wide cron service merely to pause Cacti. Wait for pollers, Spine,
script servers and other background writers to finish. Restrict web access
to the upgrade administrator and pause plugin jobs that write to Cacti.
Flush pending RRD-cache writes and confirm that the database and RRD files
remain unchanged before taking the backup.



* Use the dump client appropriate to the installed MySQL or MariaDB server.
The following example requires an existing, protected backup directory and a
database account with the server's required backup privileges. Replace the
account, database name and filename for your installation. Use its actual
database host, port and TLS options.


mysqldump --user=YOUR_BACKUP_USER --password \
  --lock-tables --quick --routines --events --triggers \
  --databases cacti --result-file=/protected/backup/cacti-YYYYMMDD.sql



The password is prompted. With all Cacti writers paused, this dump
method <https://dev.mysql.com/doc/refman/8.0/en/mysqldump.html> uses table locks for the Cacti database, including
nontransactional tables. A transaction-only dump does not cover those
tables. Verify the command succeeded and test restoring the dump separately;
a nonempty file alone is not proof of a complete backup. Use a new filename
rather than overwriting a known good backup.


* Back up the Cacti application files, RRDs and local configuration together
with the database dump. Preserve ownership, permissions and hidden files.
Include include/config.php, custom scripts, query resources, plugins,
secret files, including any alternate path_csrf_secret, and configured
paths outside the application tree. Record symbolic links and back up their
targets separately. Also record the Spine configuration, cron/service
settings, web-server settings, database grants, certificates/keys and
installed versions. Package layouts may keep configuration and data outside
/usr/local/share/cacti; inspect the installed paths rather than backing up
that directory alone. Include /var/db/cacti/rra and /var/log/cacti
when those are the configured package paths, and protect the backup directory
from other users because the configuration contains credentials.




Upgrade using the existing installation method


For a binary package installation, review the proposed package transaction
before applying it. The FreeBSD Handbook <https://docs.freebsd.org/en/books/handbook/ports/#pkgng-upgrading> describes package
upgrades. Run the following with appropriate administrator privileges:


Discover the installed flavor first:


pkg info -o -x '^cacti'
pkg update



For an installed cacti-php84 package, for example:


pkg upgrade -n cacti-php84
pkg upgrade cacti-php84
pkg info -D cacti-php84



Replace cacti-php84 with the installed package name. Keep the PHP flavor
unless a separate migration is intended. If Spine is installed, review its
upgrade and verify its version remains compatible with Cacti. See the
FreeBSD installation section <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/General-Installing-Instructions.md#freebsd> for package paths and the
net-mgmt/cacti-spine port.


Check the proposed version and dependency changes before accepting the actual
upgrade. A package repository normally supplies a release rather than
development source. Stop if that is not your approved target and select an
appropriate repository or installation method. Do not delete Cacti first or
assume that upgrading one package cannot affect its dependencies.


For a ports-managed installation, follow the handbook's ports upgrade
procedure <https://docs.freebsd.org/en/books/handbook/ports/#ports-upgrading> and applicable /usr/ports/UPDATING entries. Use the
existing ports management method; do not switch between ports and binary
packages without reviewing their options and dependencies. A source
installation instead follows Upgrading Cacti <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Upgrading-Cacti.md>.


Preserve the working configuration and merge required changes from the new
configuration example. Keep existing database credentials, collector IDs, paths
and secrets. Review package-generated configuration changes and restore local
resources only after checking compatibility. Recreate cache or data directories
only where the installed configuration requires them, with access for the
actual service account; do not assume a universal account or apply recursive
ownership changes to the whole installation.


Check development dependencies


Development source requires PHP 8.3 or later in the PHP 8 series, with the
extensions declared by the selected revision. Install locked dependencies in
the staging directory with composer install --no-dev, then run composer check-platform-reqs --no-dev. Preserve this complete dependency tree when
deploying the staged application. Do not run composer update against a live
installation. A complete release bundle can already include dependencies;
verify its platform checks instead of assuming that every archive is complete.


Complete the application upgrade


Open the existing Cacti URL as an administrator and complete its database
upgrade. Review the installer and logs before resuming collection. Upgrade
remote collectors' local files and configuration through their installation
method, complete the applicable upgrade/synchronization, and verify their
versions, connectivity and logs. Do not rely on a fixed number of poll cycles
for completion. Check the matching Spine version on collectors using Spine.


After successful checks, re-enable Data Collection Enabled and restore the
recorded Cacti cron entry or service. Verify a complete poll cycle, recent RRD
samples and representative graphs on every collector. If recovery is needed,
stop all writers again and restore the matching files, database, RRDs and
configuration as one set; restoring old application files alone does not undo a
database upgrade.

Hardening a Cacti Installation


This page covers OS-level hardening controls for a production Cacti host.
The goal is defense in depth: even if a vulnerability in Cacti's PHP code
is exploited, the attacker's reach should be limited.


Why These Controls Matter


A recurring class of vulnerability in Cacti involves writing an arbitrary
file into the resource/ or scripts/ tree and then reaching that file
over the web.  The package-import advisory (GHSA-7cmj-g5qc-pj88, CVE-2024-25641) is a
concrete example: a crafted package archive could write resource/test.php
and execute it as a web request.  The controls below close or limit that
path at the OS and web-server layers, independent of the PHP fix.




1. File Permissions


Cacti writes to three paths at runtime.  Everything else should be owned
by root (or a non-web user) and not writable by the web process.





	Path
	Purpose
	Recommended mode






	rra/
	RRDtool data files
	www-data:www-data 750




	log/
	Cacti log files
	www-data:www-data 750




	cache/
	Boost / poller cache
	www-data:www-data 750




	resource/
	XML templates
	root:root 755 (read-only for web)




	scripts/
	Data-source scripts
	root:root 755 (read-only for web)




	lib/, include/
	PHP libraries
	root:root 755





Apply once after installation:


# Writable paths (web user only)
chown -R www-data:www-data /var/www/html/cacti/rra \
                           /var/www/html/cacti/log \
                           /var/www/html/cacti/cache
chmod -R 750              /var/www/html/cacti/rra \
                           /var/www/html/cacti/log \
                           /var/www/html/cacti/cache

# Everything else read-only for the web user
chown -R root:root         /var/www/html/cacti
chmod -R 755               /var/www/html/cacti
# Re-apply writable exceptions after the recursive chmod
chown -R www-data:www-data /var/www/html/cacti/rra \
                           /var/www/html/cacti/log \
                           /var/www/html/cacti/cache

# Protect the database credentials in config.php.  The recursive 755 above
# leaves it world-readable; restrict it to root and the web user.
chown root:www-data /var/www/html/cacti/include/config.php
chmod 640           /var/www/html/cacti/include/config.php






Note: The scripts/ and resource/ paths may be written during
template import or package import by a privileged CLI process running
as root, not by the web process.  If your workflow requires the web
process to write there, use a strict code-review policy for any import
and consider the compat profile instead.





2. Web Server — Block Direct Access to Scripts and Resources


PHP files inside scripts/ and resource/ must not be reachable via HTTP.
These directories hold data-source scripts and XML templates; a web request
to a .php file written there is the direct exploitation path.


Apache


<DirectoryMatch> is only valid in server config or <VirtualHost> context,
not .htaccess. Add this inside your Cacti <VirtualHost>:


# Deny direct HTTP access to the script and resource trees.
# A PHP file written here via import cannot be reached over the web.
<DirectoryMatch "^/var/www/html/cacti/(scripts|resource)/">
    <FilesMatch "\.php$">
        Require all denied
    </FilesMatch>
</DirectoryMatch>



If you only have access to .htaccess (e.g. shared hosting), use a
FilesMatch block inside each directory instead:


# scripts/.htaccess and resource/.htaccess
<FilesMatch "\.php$">
    Require all denied
</FilesMatch>



Or, more broadly, deny all non-XML/non-script files from direct access:


<Directory "/var/www/html/cacti/scripts">
    Require all denied
</Directory>

<Directory "/var/www/html/cacti/resource">
    <FilesMatch "\.php$">
        Require all denied
    </FilesMatch>
</Directory>



Nginx


Add inside your server {} block:


# Block PHP execution in scripts/ and resource/
location ~ ^/cacti/(scripts|resource)/.*\.php$ {
    return 403;
}





3. PHP-FPM Pool Hardening


PHP itself warns that php-fpm pools are not a true security boundary, but
they provide useful knobs.  Create a dedicated pool for Cacti
(/etc/php/8.x/fpm/pool.d/cacti.conf):


[cacti]
user  = www-data
group = www-data

listen = /run/php/php-cacti.sock
listen.owner = www-data
listen.group = www-data

; Drop all inherited environment variables — pass only what Cacti needs.
clear_env = yes

; Only execute .php files — blocks .phtml, .php3, .phar, etc.
security.limit_extensions = .php

; Disable dangerous functions at the pool level (not overridable by ini_set).
; Cacti's web process shells out to RRDtool, fping and helper binaries via
; exec, shell_exec, system, popen and proc_open (see lib/rrd.php, lib/ping.php,
; lib/spikekill.php), so disabling those breaks graph rendering and diagnostics.
; Only passthru and pcntl_exec are unused by the web tier and safe to disable.
; For tighter control, constrain the process with open_basedir and a hardened
; PATH rather than removing the shell-exec family Cacti depends on.
php_admin_value[disable_functions] = passthru,pcntl_exec

; Restrict file operations to the Cacti tree.
; NOTE: open_basedir disables the PHP realpath cache.  Measure the
; performance impact before enabling in production.  It is an extra
; safety net, not a full security boundary (PHP documentation).
; php_admin_value[open_basedir] = /var/www/html/cacti:/tmp

; Suppress PHP version disclosure.
php_admin_flag[expose_php] = Off



Reload php-fpm after changes:


systemctl reload php8.x-fpm   # Debian/Ubuntu
systemctl reload php-fpm       # RHEL/Rocky/Alma





4. SELinux (RHEL / AlmaLinux / Rocky Linux)


SELinux ships enforcing on RHEL-family systems.  These commands apply
the correct file contexts so Apache can serve Cacti while limiting what
the web process can write.


File Contexts


CACTI=/var/www/html/cacti

# Read-only web content (Apache can read, not write)
semanage fcontext -a -t httpd_sys_content_t      "${CACTI}(/.*)?"
semanage fcontext -a -t httpd_sys_content_t      "${CACTI}/scripts(/.*)?"
semanage fcontext -a -t httpd_sys_content_t      "${CACTI}/resource(/.*)?"

# Read-write content for runtime paths only
semanage fcontext -a -t httpd_sys_rw_content_t   "${CACTI}/rra(/.*)?"
semanage fcontext -a -t httpd_sys_rw_content_t   "${CACTI}/log(/.*)?"
semanage fcontext -a -t httpd_sys_rw_content_t   "${CACTI}/cache(/.*)?"

# Apply the contexts
restorecon -Rv "${CACTI}"



Database Network Access


If your database server is reached over TCP (not a local Unix socket),
enable the SELinux boolean that permits it:


setsebool -P httpd_can_network_connect_db on



Poller Systemd Drop-in


The Cacti spine poller runs as a separate daemon and may need to connect
outward for SNMP.  Do not force it into the httpd_t domain: that confines
spine to Apache's policy rather than freeing it, and outbound access in
httpd_t is still gated by booleans.  If SNMP polling is blocked by SELinux,
enable the relevant boolean instead and leave the daemon in its own domain:


setsebool -P httpd_can_network_connect on



Run systemctl daemon-reload && systemctl restart cactid after changes.





Verify: After applying contexts, confirm Apache can still serve pages
and the poller can poll.  Use audit2why on any AVC denials in
/var/log/audit/audit.log.





5. AppArmor (Debian / Ubuntu)


Ubuntu Server ships AppArmor enforcing by default.  Create a profile for
the Cacti php-fpm pool at /etc/apparmor.d/cacti-fpm:


#include <tunables/global>

profile cacti-fpm /usr/sbin/php-fpm* {
    #include <abstractions/base>
    #include <abstractions/php>
    #include <abstractions/mysql>

    # Cacti tree — read-only by default
    /var/www/html/cacti/           r,
    /var/www/html/cacti/**         r,

    # Allow execution of Cacti PHP files
    /var/www/html/cacti/*.php      r,
    /var/www/html/cacti/**/*.php   r,

    # Runtime write paths
    /var/www/html/cacti/rra/**     rw,
    /var/www/html/cacti/log/**     rw,
    /var/www/html/cacti/cache/**   rw,

    # Helper binaries Cacti shells out to.  AppArmor is default-deny once
    # file rules are present, so these need explicit execute (ix) or graphs
    # and polling break under enforce mode.
    /usr/bin/rrdtool               ix,
    /usr/bin/fping                 ix,
    /usr/bin/ping                  ix,
    /usr/bin/snmpget               ix,
    /usr/bin/snmpbulkwalk          ix,
    /usr/bin/php                   ix,

    # Temp files
    /tmp/**                        rw,
    /var/tmp/**                    rw,

    # Deny write to scripts/ and resource/
    deny /var/www/html/cacti/scripts/**  w,
    deny /var/www/html/cacti/resource/** w,
}



Load and enforce the profile:


apparmor_parser -r /etc/apparmor.d/cacti-fpm
aa-enforce /etc/apparmor.d/cacti-fpm



Attach the Profile to the php-fpm Service


Add a drop-in at /etc/systemd/system/php8.x-fpm.service.d/apparmor.conf:


[Service]
AppArmorProfile=cacti-fpm



Reload and restart:


systemctl daemon-reload
systemctl restart php8.x-fpm



Use complain mode (aa-complain) first to test without blocking, then
switch to enforce once logs show no legitimate denials:


# Test mode
aa-complain /etc/apparmor.d/cacti-fpm

# Enforce when ready
aa-enforce  /etc/apparmor.d/cacti-fpm





6. Verification Checklist


After applying all controls, confirm:


* [ ] curl -s http://localhost/cacti/scripts/ss_webseer.php returns 403

* [ ] curl -s http://localhost/cacti/resource/ returns 403 for any .php inside

* [ ] The Cacti web UI loads and graphs render (rra/ is writable)

* [ ] The poller runs without errors (tail -f /var/www/html/cacti/log/cacti.log)

* [ ] On RHEL: no AVC denials in ausearch -m avc -ts recent

* [ ] On Debian/Ubuntu: no AppArmor denials in journalctl -k | grep apparmor

* Installing Under Ubuntu/Debian <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Installing-Under-Ubuntu-Debian.md>

* Installing Under Enterprise Linux (LAMP) <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Install-Under-Enterprise-Linux-LAMP.md>

* Standards Security <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Standards-Security.md>

Configuring SSL for Cacti


Enabling HTTPS for Cacti is done at the web server level. This page covers
self-signed certificates for internal use and Let's Encrypt for public-facing
servers.





Note: If using multiple Data Collectors, all must have HTTPS enabled for
the remote polling feature to work correctly.



Self-Signed Certificate (Internal Use)


Self-signed certificates are suitable for internal networks where you control
the clients. Use ECDSA (secp384r1) for best performance, or RSA 4096 if your
environment requires RSA.


ECDSA (recommended)


# RHEL/Rocky/AlmaLinux
dnf install -y mod_ssl

# Generate a 384-bit EC private key and self-signed certificate (valid 3 years)
openssl req -x509 -nodes -newkey ec -pkeyopt ec_paramgen_curve:P-384 \
    -keyout /etc/pki/tls/private/cacti.key \
    -out /etc/pki/tls/certs/cacti.crt \
    -days 1095 \
    -subj "/CN=cacti.example.com" \
    -addext "subjectAltName=DNS:cacti.example.com"

chmod 600 /etc/pki/tls/private/cacti.key



RSA (alternative)


# 4096-bit RSA — use when EC is not supported by your TLS client pool
openssl req -x509 -nodes -newkey rsa:4096 \
    -keyout /etc/pki/tls/private/cacti.key \
    -out /etc/pki/tls/certs/cacti.crt \
    -days 1095 \
    -subj "/CN=cacti.example.com" \
    -addext "subjectAltName=DNS:cacti.example.com"

chmod 600 /etc/pki/tls/private/cacti.key






Debian/Ubuntu paths: the commands above use the RHEL layout
(/etc/pki/tls/...). On Debian/Ubuntu, write the key and certificate to
/etc/ssl/private/cacti.key and /etc/ssl/certs/cacti.crt instead, to match
the Apache SSLCertificateKeyFile/SSLCertificateFile paths shown below.






Key size: RSA 2048-bit is below NIST SP 800-131A Rev 2 guidance for
long-term use. Use 4096-bit RSA or an ECDSA key.



Apache Configuration


Update /etc/httpd/conf.d/ssl.conf (RHEL/Rocky/AlmaLinux) or
/etc/apache2/sites-available/cacti-ssl.conf (Debian/Ubuntu):


<VirtualHost *:443>
    ServerName cacti.example.com
    DocumentRoot /var/www/html/cacti

    SSLEngine on
    # RHEL/Rocky/AlmaLinux cert paths:
    #   SSLCertificateFile    /etc/pki/tls/certs/cacti.crt
    #   SSLCertificateKeyFile /etc/pki/tls/private/cacti.key
    # Debian/Ubuntu cert paths:
    SSLCertificateFile    /etc/ssl/certs/cacti.crt
    SSLCertificateKeyFile /etc/ssl/private/cacti.key

    # Require TLS 1.2 or 1.3; TLS 1.0 and 1.1 are prohibited (RFC 8996)
    SSLProtocol           -all +TLSv1.2 +TLSv1.3

    # TLS 1.2 cipher suites -- no RC4, 3DES, or export ciphers
    SSLCipherSuite        ECDHE-ECDSA-AES128-GCM-SHA256:ECDHE-RSA-AES128-GCM-SHA256:\
ECDHE-ECDSA-AES256-GCM-SHA384:ECDHE-RSA-AES256-GCM-SHA384:\
ECDHE-ECDSA-CHACHA20-POLY1305:ECDHE-RSA-CHACHA20-POLY1305
    SSLHonorCipherOrder   on

    # TLS 1.3 cipher suites (SSLCipherSuite does not apply to TLS 1.3)
    SSLOpenSSLConfCmd     Ciphersuites TLS_AES_256_GCM_SHA384:TLS_CHACHA20_POLY1305_SHA256:TLS_AES_128_GCM_SHA256
</VirtualHost>

# Optional: redirect HTTP to HTTPS (uncomment to enable)
#<VirtualHost *:80>
#    ServerName cacti.example.com
#    Redirect permanent / https://cacti.example.com/
#</VirtualHost>



Restart Apache after editing:


apachectl configtest
systemctl restart httpd        # RHEL/Rocky/AlmaLinux
systemctl restart apache2      # Debian/Ubuntu



Let's Encrypt (Public-Facing Servers)


For servers reachable from the internet, use Let's Encrypt for a
browser-trusted certificate at no cost.


RHEL/Rocky/AlmaLinux


# certbot ships in EPEL on RHEL/Rocky/AlmaLinux, not in BaseOS/AppStream
dnf install -y epel-release
dnf install -y certbot python3-certbot-apache
certbot --apache -d cacti.example.com



Debian/Ubuntu


apt-get install -y certbot python3-certbot-apache
certbot --apache -d cacti.example.com



Certbot configures Apache and sets up automatic renewal. Verify auto-renewal
works:


certbot renew --dry-run



Verifying the Configuration


After restarting Apache, confirm TLS is working and that weak protocols are
disabled:


# Check the certificate and protocol support
openssl s_client -connect cacti.example.com:443 -tls1_2 < /dev/null
openssl s_client -connect cacti.example.com:443 -tls1   < /dev/null  # should fail



For a comprehensive scan, use SSL Labs <https://www.ssllabs.com/ssltest/> on
public servers or testssl.sh on internal hosts:


# testssl.sh (download from https://testssl.sh/)
./testssl.sh cacti.example.com

Version Specific Release Notes


Summary


Below you will find the release note for all versions of Cacti 1.0.0 and above.
Release notes for earlier Cacti versions have been excluded due to the large
list of changes that have occurred over the years, and since Cacti 0.8.x is no
longer supported, it's a good time to for the purposes of this document, move
them aside.


Before that list, let's talks about Cacti 1.0.0 first.


Cacti 1.0.0


This was the initial release of Cacti 1.x. With the release of Cacti 1.0.0 many
improvements and enhancements have been made. As part of ongoing efforts to
improve Cacti almost 20 plugins were merged into the core of Cacti eliminating
the need for the plugins. A major refresh of the interface has been started and
will continue to occur as development on Cacti continues.


Plugins integrated into Cacti Core


The following plugins have been merged into the core Cacti code as of version
1.0.0:





	Plugin
	Description






	snmpagent
	An SNMP Agent extension, trap and notification generator




	clog
	Log viewers for administrators




	settings
	Core plugin providing email and DNS services




	boost
	Large system performance boost plugin




	dsstats
	Cacti data source statistics




	watermark
	Watermark graphs




	ssl
	Force https connection




	ugroup
	User groups support




	domains
	Multiple authentication domains




	jqueryskin
	User interface skinning




	secpass
	C3 level password and site security




	logrotate
	Log management




	realtime
	Realtime graphing




	rrdclean
	RRDfile maintenance




	nectar
	Email based graph reporting




	aggregate
	Templating, creation and management of aggregate graphs




	autom8
	Graph and Tree creation automation




	discovery
	Network Discovery and Device automation




	spikekill
	Removes spikes from Graphs




	superlinks
	Allows administrators to links to additional sites

	security[issue#]

Navigating the Cacti User Interface


Menu names and available tabs depend on your Cacti version, permissions,
theme, and installed plugins. The screenshots illustrate navigation and may
show an earlier theme or release.


The Cacti User Interface is visually broken into multiple panels.  Each major
panel is designed to hold content.  Depending on the Cacti Theme you use
some of these panel may not be visible at the Theme Developers discretion.  The
common panels are:


* Top Tabs - Where you perform major navigation separate content

* Navigation Area - If the content in navigated to by the Top Tab has a
menu,
it will be found here.

* Breadcrumb Bar - This is where you can see where in the Cacti interface
you
are currently pointed to

* Content Area - This is where you present tables, charts, forms, etc.  It
is
where your main Content resides

* Footer - This section is left for Theme Developers to insert content
that belongs at the bottom of the screen.



You can see the default Cacti layout with its various panels in the image below.
You will note, in this Modern Theme, the Theme Author has decided to dispense
with the Footer.


[Image: Cacti Layout] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/navigation-layout.png>


In Cacti, when you click on the Top Tab, you will by default enter a
completely different section of Cacti.  Cacti's Top Tabs are designed
to mimic browser tabs, this helps with users orienting themselves to the
various sections of Cacti.  When you have many Plugins installed as
in the example above, you can see clearly the benefit of these
navigation aids.


Inside each of these panels, a page can be broken into sub-panels.  Two
panels customarily broken into sub-panels include the Navigation Area
and the Content Area.


In the example below, we show the Device page in Cacti calling out the
various sub-panels.


[Image: Cacti Layout Subpanels] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/navigation-layout-subpanel.png>


Most of Cacti's pages are laid out in this fashion.  However, what goes into
the Cacti Content Area is completely under the Plugin authors
control.


At the Theme developers discretion, all pages should include both the Top
Tab
and Breadcrumb Bar.  Inside of the Breadcrumb Bar or Top Tab panels
you should always see the User Profile and Menu on the right.


To use Cacti properly, you should first understand these sections.  We will
start by describing the Cacti Console.


Cacti Core Panels and Sub-Panels


* Top Tabs


Cacti Top Tabs provide Cacti with multiple Navigation Areas.  By
default, Cacti includes four Top Tabs.  They are Console, Graphs,
Log and Reports.


* Breadcrumbs


Breadcrumbs appear directly below the Top Tabs.  Note that some Cacti
Themes disable the Breadcrumbs.  You can click on a Breadcrumb area
to navigate to that area if desired.


* Cacti Content Area


This is where the main page content will be displayed.  It is directly below
the Breadcrumbs or the Top Tabs with some Cacti Themes.  They can
include any HTML that the Plugin Author or Cacti Administrator desires in
the case of External Links.


* Navigation Menu


If you click on the Cacti Console, you will see an example Navigation
Menu.  These menus can appear on any Plugin based Top Tab page in
addition to the Cacti Console.


* Cacti Tables


These tables are where table based data is rendered in Cacti.  Cacti
Tables are presented using an arcane, though easy to use API.


* Table Filters


Any Cacti Table can include a Table Filters.  These filters can be
used to limit the data returned to a Cacti Table.


* Actions Dropdown


Any page that includes a Cacti Table will generally include an Actions
Dropdown.  These Actions Dropdown menus allow you to take action on a
table row or rows.


* User Profile and Menu


This is where a Cacti User can edit their profile, change their password,
logout, or find links to other Cacti information and support.




Non-Administrative users, such as the Cacti Guest account should not have
access to the Cacti Console.  The Cacti Guest account should additionally
not have access to their User Profile as that account is shared with
many users.


The Cacti Graphs Top Tab


The Cacti Graphs Top Tab is where most Cacti Graphs are viewed.  By
default, the Cacti Graphs Top Tab includes three distinct views.  They
include:


* Tree View


Allows Cacti Users to view Graphs in the form of hierarchical Trees.
These Trees are generally constructed by the Cacti Administrator and are
controlled either at the User or User Group level.


* Preview View


The Preview View provides a view of all Graphs that a Cacti User has
access to.  Table Filters are provided to constrain the list of Graphs
returned to the page.


* List View


The List View allows the Cacti user to Create their own Preview Page
by allowing them to select graphs from various pages, and then finally view
those pages from the Preview View.




In the example Tree View page below, you can see the Tree Navigation
Area to the left, and in the Cacti Content Area, you can see the
Graphs and a Table Filter area for constraining the list of Graphs
returned.  You can Search the Tree View from the Search area above the
Tree Navigation Area.


[Image: Cacti Tree View Page] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/tree-view-page.png>


The Cacti Console


In the image below, you can see a basic Cacti Console menu area.  It is divided
into separate sub-menus.  We will describe the purpose of each next.


[Image: Cacti Console] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/navigation-console.png>


* Main Console - This sub-menu pick is fairly benign.  It provides
an open area.  This screen feels like it needs more content if it's
the Main Console. Fortunately, Plugin developers have solved
this problem.  The intropage plugin for example can fulfill that need.

* Create - This sub-menu allows you to create both Devices and
Graphs.  They are essentially shortcuts to other sub-menu picks.

* Management - This is where all core Cacti Site, Graph,
Device, Tree, Data Source, and Aggregate non-templated
objects reside.  When you install Cacti Plugins, you will find
they extend this sub-menu.

* Data Collection - This is where you define rules for Data Collection
Examples include: Data Collectors, Data Input Methods and
Data Queries

* Templates - This is where you find all of Cacti's various Templates
outside of Automation.  By default, You will find Templates for
Graphs, Data Sources, Devices, Aggregates, and Colors.

* Automation - This sub-menu is where you find rules for automating
Device Discovery, and Rules for creating Devices, Graphs,
and Trees.

* Presets - This area contains meta objects that cross Template
boundaries and are Global in nature.  They include; Data Source Profiles,
CDEFs, VDEFs, GPrint Presets, and Colors

* Import Export - This is how you can import and export various Cacti
Template objects.

* Configuration - This is where you manage Users, User Groups,
Login Providers, Global Settings, and Plugins on the development
branch. Cacti 1.2 has User Domains instead.

* Utilities - This is where Cacti includes common utilities that can be use
in the Web Portal without having to goto the Command line.

* Troubleshooting - There are some handy utilities here that help
diagnose common problems with Cacti.



All of these objects types will be explained in subsequent sections of the
Cacti documentation.  For now, it's important just to know that these pages
exist.




Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


Principles of Operation


Cacti collects measurements, stores them in Round Robin Database (RRD) files,
and uses RRDtool to render graphs. A MySQL or MariaDB database holds device
configuration, templates, permissions, polling state, and intermediate results.
RRD files hold the time-series measurements.


Devices and sites


A Device <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Devices.md> represents something Cacti monitors, such as a server,
switch, sensor, or service. Its configuration identifies the responsible data
collector, address, availability checks, and SNMP settings where needed.
Site <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Sites.md> assignments organize devices by location; they do not create
separate database or security boundaries.


Device Templates <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Device-Templates.md> associate graph templates and data
queries with a device. Automation <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Automation-Networks.md> can discover devices
and apply rules for creating devices, graphs, and tree entries.


Data collectors and scheduling


The main Data Collector <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Data-Collectors.md> runs on the central Cacti system.
Remote collectors poll devices closer to their location and exchange
configuration and results with the main system. During a loss of connectivity,
a configured remote collector can retain results locally for later delivery.
This does not replace backups or provide automatic failover of the main server.


An operating-system scheduler or the Cacti daemon starts poller.php.
It coordinates collection and launches the configured engine: the PHP
cmd.php poller or the C-based Spine <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Spine.md> poller. The scheduler
interval,
poller interval, and data-source profiles must agree; changing cron alone does
not change an existing RRD file's sampling interval.


Data retrieval


Cacti uses SNMP, external scripts, and resident PHP script-server functions.
The available SNMP algorithms depend on the device, Net-SNMP, PHP, and Spine
builds. Scripts execute with the poller's operating-system permissions, not
with an interactive administrator's environment.


Data Input Methods <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Data-Input-Methods.md> describe commands and their input
and output fields. Data Queries <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Data-Queries.md> enumerate indexed objects,
such as network interfaces or disk partitions. Query input fields describe
those objects; output fields provide measurements to collect.


[Image: Data Input Methods and Queries] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/data-input-method-and-query.png>


Collection and storage


Data Sources <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Data-Sources.md> connect collection definitions to RRD files and
the series stored in them. The poller writes results to database output tables.
Cacti's update processing writes those measurements to RRD files, either during
normal polling or through Boost <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Boost.md> when on-demand updating is enabled.
Spine does not directly render graphs or replace the RRD update process.


[Image: Principles of Operation] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/principles-of-operation.svg>


Data Source Profiles <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Data-Profiles.md> define sampling, heartbeat,
consolidation functions, and retention when files are created. RRD archives
have bounded storage for their configured layout. Older measurements are
consolidated or expire according to that layout; changing a graph's timespan
does not recover detail already consolidated away.


An RRD file can contain multiple named series. Database data-source counts,
poller item counts, graph counts, and RRD file counts therefore need not match.


Graphing


Graph Templates <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Graph-Templates.md> define presentation settings and graph
items. A graph item can draw a series, calculate a value, or add legend text.
Several graphs can reuse an existing data source without collecting it again.
CDEFs <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/CDEFs.md>, VDEFs <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/VDEFs.md>, and GPRINT presets <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/GPRINTs.md> control
calculations and formatting at graph-rendering time.


Users view permitted graphs through the tree, list, and preview views described
in Viewing Graphs <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Viewing-Graphs.md>. Permissions and tree publication control
which graphs a user can reach; adding a graph to a tree does not start polling.


Extensions


Plugins add features through Cacti's plugin API. Check each plugin's release
compatibility, installation instructions, and permissions before enabling it.
Template import and export share definitions; they do not transfer historical
RRD measurements. See Plugin Management <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Plugins.md> and
Template Import <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Import-Template.md>.




Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


How to Graph Your Network


Create a device, select its measurements, and verify collection before adding
its graphs to a tree. The manual workflow below is useful for a first device.
Automation <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Automation-Networks.md> can perform these tasks for larger sets of
devices once its discovery and graph rules are configured.


Creating a Device


Open Console > Management > Devices and click Add. Enter a description
and address, choose the responsible data collector, and select a suitable
Device Template <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Device-Templates.md>. The template supplies associated graph
templates and data queries; it does not restrict you to those defaults.


[Image: Adding a New Device] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/new-device.png>


Device Field Definitions


* Description identifies the device in lists and default graph titles.

* Hostname is the device's address or resolvable DNS name.

* Device Template associates collection and graph definitions.

* Poller Association determines which collector reaches the device.

* Site groups devices by location.

* Notes records local information about the device.

* Disable Device stops collection while retaining its configuration.



Availability/Reachability Options


Choose a downed-device detection method appropriate to the device. Cacti can
use SNMP, ping, both, either, or no availability test. A failed availability
check can prevent measurements from being collected even when another
protocol is reachable.


For ping checks, select the method, port where applicable, timeout, and retry
count. ICMP permissions depend on the poller engine and operating system.
Test connectivity from the collector assigned to the device.


SNMP Options


Configure the SNMP version and credentials accepted by the device:


* SNMPv3 supports authentication and privacy. Prefer authPriv with
mutually supported SHA and AES protocols where the device supports them.

* SNMPv2c supports 64-bit counters, but its community is not encrypted.
Restrict access to trusted collectors and networks.

* SNMPv1 is for legacy devices and does not support 64-bit counters.



Set the SNMP port, timeout, retries, and maximum OIDs per request. Start with
values the device supports; increase request sizes only after verifying that
responses are complete and collection remains reliable.


Security Options for SNMP V3


Choose the security level, username, authentication protocol and passphrase,
privacy protocol and passphrase, and context or engine ID when required.
The protocols offered depend on Cacti settings and the installed SNMP builds.
The development defaults use SHA authentication and AES privacy. MD5 and DES
are legacy algorithms and can be hidden by policy or unavailable in the build.


SNMP Retrieval Types


Cacti's PHP paths can use PHP's SNMP extension or external Net-SNMP tools.
The extension supports SNMPv1, v2c, and v3; Cacti can select external tools for
protocol combinations unavailable in the extension. Spine uses Net-SNMP
through its C library. Verify the actual PHP, Net-SNMP, and Spine builds rather
than assuming all offered algorithms are available on every platform.


Verify device discovery


Save the device and check the SNMP information and any error shown on the edit
page. Review Associated Data Queries and Associated Graph Templates.
If a query has no rows, run its verbose query to check connectivity, OIDs,
indexes, and credentials before creating graphs.


For interface traffic, prefer the 64-bit counter graph type when the device
and selected SNMP version support it. See Data Queries <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Data-Queries.md> for
query-to-template mappings and Device Management <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Devices.md> for other
availability and collection settings.


Creating the Graphs


Open Console > Create > New Graphs or use Create Graphs for this Device
from the device page. Select the device, then choose the graph templates or
query rows to graph. For indexed queries, choose the appropriate graph type
before clicking Create.


[Image: Creating New Graphs] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/new-graphs.png>


Fill in any per-graph values requested by the templates and complete creation.
Creating a graph defines collection; it does not immediately provide historical
measurements. Allow scheduled polling to produce enough valid samples for the
selected data-source type.


Verify and publish


* Check the device's status and the Cacti log <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Cacti-Log.md> after polling.

* Open the graph under Console > Management > Graphs. For blank graphs,
inspect Data Source Debugging <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Data-Debug.md> and the RRD last update.

* Add the graph or device to a Tree <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Trees.md>, publish the tree, and grant
the intended users access.

* Confirm the result in Viewing Graphs <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Viewing-Graphs.md> with an account
that has the intended permissions.





Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


Device Management


This section will describe Device management in Cacti.


Adding a Device to Cacti can be done in a few different ways, either via
the Web GUI, Cacti's Automation, or the Command Line Interface (CLI).


Web GUI Option


To add a device via the Web GUI first click on Console > Management > Devices
and
you will see the below device console window which will show existing devices
if any


[Image: Device Console] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/device-console-window.png>


You will now select the + on the top right hand corner


[Image: Add Device Button] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/add-device-button.png>


Once you select the + otherwise known as the add device button you will see the
below screen which will ask you for device specific information


Some of the most important information about the device will be required in this
window which includes


* Description - The name that will appear on Graphs by default

* IP/Hostname - The DNS or IP address of the actual Device. IPv6
address insert into brackets (example: [2001:db8::1])

* Poller Association - Defines which Data Collector is responsible for
pulling information about the Device

* Device Template - Cisco, Net-SNMP, Linux, etc - The Cacti object that
holds all the Graph Templates and Data Queries to be graphed

* Site, Location - Very important to performing Meta queries, or for
Site level Graph organization on Cacti Graph Trees

* Availability/Reachability - Settings that describe Device
timeouts and availability methods.

* SNMP information - SNMP Credentials for connecting to the Device

* Device Notes - Arbitrary unstructured information about the Device



[Image: Add Device Info Screen] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/add-device-screen.png>


Cacti requires this basic information to be able to monitor the device and once
entered, click save on the bottom right corner. With the device created you will
need to add graphs for the device by clicking Create graphs for this device
on the top right hand corner.


Availability/Reachability Settings


Cacti prefers to use the Simple Network Management Protocol (SNMP) to
communicate
with Devices.  Therefore, when creating a Device, you need to provide
SNMP
credentials to obtain information about the Device in order to collect data
from
it.  Before Cacti will query the Device for data, it first verifies that the
Device is up and responding.  When doing so, you have several options.
They include:


* None - Always assume the device is up.  This is generally reserved for
Device
objects that do not have a state.

* SNMP Uptime - Query the SNMP Uptime Instance OID

* Ping and SNMP Uptime - Ping the device but also check the SNMP Uptime
Instance OID

* Ping - Either ICMP, TCP at a port, or UDP as a port. Newer version has an
additional method TCP Ping Closed.
The device is considered UP even if the TCP ping returns closed

* Ping or SNMP Uptime - Only one needs to be working for Cacti to collect
data

* SNMP Desc - Query the SNMP sysDescription in cases where the SNMP Uptime
OID
is not available

* SNMP GetNext - Query the first available OID in the OID tree for the
Device
Used for certain devices that have limited SNMP support.



SNMP Credentials


When providing the SNMP credentials, Cacti currently supports the following
versions:


* Version 1 - Rarely used any more.  Reserved for very old hardware

* Version 2c - Supports 64-bit counters; community strings are not encrypted

* Version 3 - Fully supported, including advanced settings such as SHA224+
or AES192+ with SNMPv3.  Cacti automatically leverages the Net-SNMP binaries
where needed, so there is no need to uninstall the php-snmp module.



When providing the SNMP Credentials, Cacti will warn you if you have provided
incomplete information depending on the SNMP Version and SNMP Security Level
you have specified.


Additional Important Options


There are some additional options to be aware of before starting to use Cacti.
They include the following:


* Device Threads - If your device is far away, and can tolerate multiple
threads
querying information, you can increase this number to reduce the time it takes
to collect all information.

* Maximum OIDs Per Get Request - Otherwise known as MaxOID's, this SNMP
option
will allow the SNMP client to gather more metrics per get request.  Please
keep
in mind that the higher you make this number, the longer a SNMP respond may
take.
So, you have to be sensitive about the SNMP timeout as the number gets larger.
Since, by default SNMP is generally collected over UDP, you will also be
limited
in the number of responses depending on how many routers or VPN's you traverse
to reach a device.  When traversing VPN connections, many VPN's limit the MTU
to around 500 bytes, which will significantly limit how large the Max OID's
can
be.  In some cases, it may be better to deploy a Remote Data Collector
when
your device is either far way from a latency perspective, or that you must
traverse VPN's to communicate with.

* External ID - This field is normally used for Asset Tracking information
for
the Device, but it use is entirely up to the System Administrator.



Plugin Behavior


Many Cacti Plugins can and do add additional columns to the Device table in
Cacti.
Depending on the Plugin you have installed, you will find other information that
you can provide about the device including things like:


* Notification Settings - Who to notify when the Device changes state

* Criticality - How important is the device

* Failure and Recovery Counts - How long till a device is treated as truly
down.

* Ping Thresholds - What RTL is considered bad when reaching a device



Creating devices via CLI script


Run the CLI tool from the Cacti application root using the poller account and
PHP runtime configured for that installation:


php cli/add_device.php --help
php cli/add_device.php --list-host-templates



Specify the device template, collector, availability method, and SNMP options
that match the device. Omitted options can inherit the installation's defaults;
there is no universal template or SNMP version implied by a minimal command.


For example, create an initially disabled device without SNMP, then complete
its settings in the web interface:


php cli/add_device.php --description="Example device" --ip=192.0.2.15 \
  --template=0 --version=0 --avail=none --disable



Confirm the collector assignment, configure the required credentials and
availability checks, create graphs, and enable the device when ready. Do not
include real credentials in shared command output or shell history.




Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


Graph Overview


Almost everything in Cacti is somehow related to a Graph. At any time, you can
list all available Graph by clicking on Console > Management > Graphs menu
pick. While it is possible to manually create graphs through this interface,
new users should follow the instructions provided in the next chapter for
creating New Graphs in Cacti.


For users who are familiar with RRDtool <https://www.rrdtool.org/>, you will
immediately recognize that a Graph in Cacti is closely modeled after RRDtool's
graphs. This makes sense since Cacti provides a user friendly interface to
RRDtool without requiring users to understand how RRDtool works. With this in
mind, every Graph in Cacti has certain settings and at least one Graph Item
associated with it. While graph settings define the overall properties of a
Graph, the Graph Items define the data that is to be represented on the Graph.
So the Graph Items define which data to display and how it should displayed,
and also define what should be displayed on the legend.


Each Graph and Graph Item has a set of parameters which control various aspects
of the Graph. Fortunately through the use of Graph Templates, it is not
necessary to understand the function of each field to create Graphs for your
network. When you are ready to take on the task of creating your own Graph
Templates, extensive field descriptions for both Graphs and
Graph Items are provided in that section of the manual.


Below, you can see a simple version of the Graph Management interface found
by going to Console > Management > Graphs


The screenshots below contain example devices and historical data from an
earlier interface. Check menu labels against your installed version.


[Image: Graph Management] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/graphs.png>


From this interface, you can see the Name, and ID of the Graph,
the Source Graph Template, and it's size.  At the bottom of the page, there
is a list of Action that can be taken on Graphs.  These actions are fairly
extensive, and are also extended as you add Plugins to Cacti.


When you click on the Name of the Graph, you will be taken to a Graph Edit
page as shown below.


[Image: Graph Management Edit] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/graphs-edit.png>


In most cases, where the Graph is owned by a Graph Template, there is
not much your can do on this page.  However, in the case that a Graph
is not managed by a template as in the image below, there are many more
options you will have when working with the Graph.  The downside of
not leveraging a Graph Template for your Graphs is that you have
to duplicate the work for each Graph created.


[Image: Graph Management Edit] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/graphs-edit-nontemplate.png>


When viewing a Non-Templated Graph, you have complete control of the
Graphs Canvas and all the Graph Items as you would in a Graph Template,
and the Graph Configuration as shown below.


[Image: Graph Management Edit] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/graphs-edit-nontemplate-configuration.png>


The settings in both the Canvas and Configuration components of the Graph
will be covered in the Graph Template section of the manual and not
covered here.




Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


Graph Management


This section will describe Graph management in Cacti.


Cacti features a way to view the graphs per device via the console. This allows
the administrator to view the graphs that are attached to a specific device. You
can also search by type of graph. Below we search for graphs that are
associated with the local linux server


[Image: graph management] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/graph-management.png>


Clicking on one of the graphs in the list shows the below menu. From this menu
you can enable debugging on the specific graph you can also change the template
of the graph amongst other things


[Image: Graph management click] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/graph-management-graph.png>


Modifying the graph template


An individual graph inherits managed settings from its graph template. Editing
that graph does not update the template or other graphs. Fields marked
Use Per-Graph Value in the template can be set independently on each graph.


To change shared settings, edit the Graph Template <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Graph-Templates.md> under
Console > Templates > Graphs. Review the affected graphs first: template
changes propagate to attached graphs for fields managed by that template.


[Image: Graph template options] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/graph-template-options.png>




Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


Viewing Graphs


Background


Cacti, when it was first invented in 2001 by Ian Berry, his vision was to make it
the fastest and easiest way to view and render Network Monitoring Graphs for
people in the Network, Site, and Data Center Operations Space.  As such, it's focus
from the very beginning was on render just one thing, Graphs.  So, as such,
the second Top Tab on a Cacti system is the Graphs tab.


The Graphs Top Tab


The Graphs Top Tab has a few personalities.  They include:


* Tree View


Allows Cacti Users to view Graphs in the form of hierarchical Trees.
These Trees are generally constructed by the Cacti Administrator and are
controlled either at the User or User Group level.


* Preview View


The Preview View provides a view of all Graphs that a Cacti User has
access to.  Table Filters are provided to constrain the list of Graphs
returned to the page.


* List View


The List View allows the Cacti user to Create their own Preview Page
by allowing them to select graphs from various pages, and then finally view
those pages from the Preview View.




The way these various personalities appear in Cacti is somewhat different
depending on your Theme.  Review the two images below, for how you navigate to
the various Graph View modes.


In this first image, we see the way the Graph View options are displayed to
the end user.  This is the layout of the Classic, Modern and Dark themes.


[Image: Graph View Classic] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/graph-view-classic-mode.png>


In this second image, you can see the way the Graph View page will appear for
users of the Paw, Paper-Plane, and Sunrise themes.


[Image: Graph View Alternate] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/graph-view-alternate-mode.png>


Graph Manipulation and Options


Once you can view the Graphs, there will be a Filter Panel that will provide
you multiple options to limit your view of them.  Below, you can see an image of
that Filter Panel.


[Image: Graph View Filter Sub-Panel] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/graph-view-filter-sub-panel.png>


From this sub-panel, you can do the following:


* Search by regular expression

* Select one to many, or all Graph Templates to view

* Specify the number of Graphs per Page

* Specify the number of columns to Display

* View either Legendless Thumbnails or full Graphs with their legends

* Set various Preset Time-spans, or select a Time Range

* Shift the Time either forward or backward by the Shift Time-span



Once you have the Filter where you like it from a Columns, Thumbnails, and Graphs
per Page setting, you can press the Save button, if you have the correct
permissions and save those defaults for your next login.


Depending again on permissions, to the right of the Graphs, you will find a
number of action icons that allow you to operate on the Graphs


The image below shows what that might look like if you have the Thold and
QuickTree Plugins installed.


[Image: Graph View Action Icons] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/graph-view-action-icons.png>


From top to bottom, the Graph Action Icons do the following.





	Name
	Description






	Graph Details
	Allow you to perform precision zooming, view RRDtool debug information and perform CSV exports of your Graph Data.




	CSV Export
	Allows you to directly CSV Export your Graph Data




	Realtime View
	Allows you to view your Graphs at down to a 1 second granularity either in place, or in a pop-up Window




	Spike Kill
	Allow you to clear up Spikes and Gaps in your Graph




	Create Threshold
	Create a Threshold for the Graph




	Add to QuickTree
	Allow you to select Graphs to add to Trees by simply tooling around the Graphs page.





Graph Zooming


Cacti also has a powerful Graph Zoom interface built in.  You can discover
what it allows you to do by simply right clicking in the Graph area.  When
zooming, you will zoom all the Graphs on a page.  It's quite powerful.


[Image: Graph Zoom Context Menu] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/graph-view-zoom-context-menu.png>


There are many options for the Zoom menu.  Instead of explaining them here
try them out for yourself.




Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


Creating a Graph for a Single SNMP OID


When dealing with SNMP-enabled Devices, there are often times when you want
to graph the value of a single OID. This tutorial explains how to do this in
Cacti. It also assumes that you have the "SNMP - Generic OID Template" Graph
Template which is available in the Generic SNMP Device Package. If you do
not see this Graph Template listed under Graph Templates, install the
Generic SNMP Device Package using the import_package.php command line
script located in Cacti's cli directory.


To start the process of creating a new graph for your OID, click go to
Console > Create > New Graphs, and from there find the Device you would
like to add the Graph to.


From the image below, you can see the New Graphs interface.


[Image: Graphs New] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/graphs-new.png>


In this image, only the Graph Template based Graph Templates are
displayed. Had the Graph Types been switched to any of the Data Query
based Graph Types the interface would look a bit different.


From that Interface, to create a Generic SNMP OID based Graph, you need to
first select it from the Select a graph type to create drop-down. Once you
have selected it, you may press the Create button, at which point in time, you
will see the image below.


[Image: Graphs New] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/graphs-new-generic-oid.png>


From this interface, you must define various attributes for your resulting
Graph as shown in the table below.


Table 10-1. Field Description: SNMP - Generic OID Template





	Name
	Description






	(Graph) Title
	The title that is to be used for the new graph. It is generally a good idea to keep host_description in the title, as to make the graph easier to identify later.




	(Graph) Vertical Label
	The text that will be printed along the y-axis of the graph. It is generally used to describe units, such as 'bytes' or 'percent'.




	(Graph Items) Legend Color
	The color that will be used to represent the data on the graph.




	(Graph Items) Opacity/Alpha Channel
	This optionally provides an opacity (not available with RRDtool-1.0.x) for a colored item.




	(Graph Items) Legend Text
	The text that will be used to describe the data on the graph legend.




	(Data Source) Name
	The title that is to be used for the new data source. It is generally a good idea to keep host_description in the title, as to make the data source easier to identify later.




	(Data Source) Maximum Value [snmp_oid]
	The maximum value that will be accepted from the OID. Make sure you choose a value that is reasonable for the data you are trying to graph because anything larger than the maximum will be ignored. If you are graphing a percentage, you should use '100' as the value should never exceed this.




	(Data Source) Data Source Type [snmp_oid]
	How the data from the OID should be stored by RRDtool and interpreted on the graph. If the value of the OID represents the actual data, you should use GAUGE for this field. If the OID value is a constantly incremented number, you should use COUNTER for this field. The two remaining field values, DERIVE and ABSOLUTE can be ignored in most situations.




	(Custom Data) OID
	The actual SNMP OID to graph. It is typically a good idea to enter the number OID here as opposed to using MIB names. For instance, to get the number of open files on a Netware server, you would use ".1.3.6.1.4.1.23.2.28.2.7.0" as the OID.





So, based upon your specific OID that you are graphing, You will need to fill
out these fields before the new graph can be created properly.


When finished filling in values for these fields, click the Create button. Your
new Graph can now be accessed through the Console > Management > Graphs
menu pick.





NOTE: When comparing the Table above, to the image, you will see that the
Opacity/Alpha Channel pick is missing from the interface. To add it, you
simply need > to edit the Generic SNMP OID > Graph Template, add a new
Graph Item Input override to the Alpha/Opacity on the Graph Template as
shown below. That topic will be covered in the Graph Template section of
the manual though.



[Image: Graphs New] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/graphs-new-generic-oid-alpha.png>





Important Note:


When graphing single OID's with Cacti, to ensure Spine and cmd.php will
process single OID's correctly, they should be written using numeric format
separated by dots, such as 1.3.6.1.4.1.9.9.97.


At this time, ASN's (named OID's) such as enterprise.9.9.97 (Cisco Switch)
or enterprises.cisco.ciscoMgnt.ciscoSwitchEngineMIB are not supported.





Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


Site Management

site/location

Once a site has been created, you can associate devices with it when adding
or editing a device:


[Image: cacti add device site] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/add-device-site.png>
