Create Packages


A Cacti package is a signed .xml.gz file containing template definitions and
selected scripts or query resources. This development-branch interface is
separate from ordinary XML export <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Export-Template.md>. Cacti 1.2 has
installer and CLI package import support, but not these package-management
screens.


Prepare the author and signing keys


Run cli/genkey.php --help with PHP from the Cacti installation to review the
options for that version. For a new author identity, an example is:


php cli/genkey.php --generate \
  --author="Template Author" \
  --homepage="https://example.org/templates" \
  --email="templates@example.org" \
  --country=US --state=Example --org=Example --unit=Monitoring --days=365



Replace the example metadata with the author's details. The tool writes the
signing material and author information into the configured PKI directory.
package.key is private; package.pem is the certificate, package.pub is
the public key, and package.info contains author metadata. Allow the
packaging process to read what it needs, keep the private key inaccessible
through HTTP, and back up the author identity securely. Do not publish the
private key.


Reuse the existing identity when publishing an update. --replace replaces
signing material; it is not an ordinary package-update step. Changing keys also
changes the identity recipients must verify and trust. See Package Public
Keys <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Public-Keys.md>.


Build the package


* Open Console > Import/Export > Create Packages.

* Choose the object type and exact template to export. Select Include
Dependencies when the recipient needs its referenced templates, input
methods, queries, profiles, CDEFs and VDEFs.

* Complete the description, license, version, class, tags and installation
instructions. The package version is the author's version, not the Cacti XML
hash compatibility code.

* Inspect the dependent files and include the scripts/query resources required
by the template. Check any dependencies that cannot be discovered from paths
in the template; an external agent, credentials or device configuration is
not supplied merely by including a script.

* Submit the package and check for export errors. Download the resulting
signed .xml.gz file.



The Device Templates screen can also Download a package or Archive its
current definition with a note. Those actions appear when the package public
key exists; successful packaging also needs the matching signing material and
author information. An archive is a saved package definition, not a backup of
collected RRD data.


Verify before distribution


Test the package in a separate Cacti installation using the recipient's
release. Follow Import Packages <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Import-Packages.md>, review the signer and
selected files, and confirm the templates, scripts and queries are present.
Then create a graph for a device exposing the required measurements and verify
collected values. A valid signature does not establish device or firmware
compatibility.


Distribute the package with its author version, required Cacti/runtime
versions, external setup instructions and independently verifiable signer
information. For repository distribution, see Package
Repositories <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Package-Repositories.md>.




Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


Package Repositories


A package repository supplies a package.manifest and package files to the
Import Packages interface. On the development branch, manage locations
under Console > Import/Export > Repositories. These management screens are
separate from Cacti 1.2's installer and CLI package-import workflow.


Add a repository


Create a repository and give it a recognizable name. Choose its type:


Table 1. Repository types





	Type
	Location and additional settings






	GitHub Based
	The repository URL and an explicit branch. Use the optional API key only if access requires it.




	File Based
	The directory containing the manifest and packages on the Cacti server, not a path on your browser's computer.




	Direct URL
	The base URL serving the manifest and packages.





For a GitHub-based location, use a repository URL such as
https://github.com/Cacti/packages/ and a branch that actually contains the
manifest. Do not enter a browser URL for an individual file. The importer looks
for package.manifest at the selected repository location/branch.


For a direct URL, use the repository's base location so that the manifest and
referenced packages resolve beneath it. Use HTTPS where available. For a file
location, make the directory readable by the Cacti service account. Keep any
repository API credentials private and out of exported templates or examples.


Enable the repository to make it available in Import Packages. Mark it as
the default only if it should be selected initially. Save and inspect the
reachability/manifest result; a reachable host without the manifest is not a
usable repository.


Import and maintain


Open Import Packages <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Import-Packages.md>, select the enabled location, and
review available packages, signer identities and the proposed changes.
Repository access and package-signature trust are separate decisions. Merely
adding a URL does not establish that every package author should be trusted.


The Actions menu can enable, disable, delete, or select a default repository.
Disabling a location stops offering it for imports; it does not remove
templates or files previously imported from it. If a manifest or download
becomes unavailable, check the configured branch/path, credentials and
service-account access before retrying. Do not replace a failed signed download
with an edited payload.


Package authors should use Create Packages <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Create-Packages.md> and follow the
repository's manifest format and publication procedure when adding a release.
Record package versions and required external setup; a repository listing is
not a device-certification matrix.




Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


Package Public Keys


The development branch's Console > Import/Export > Public Keys screen
manages accepted package-signing identities. A public key verifies a package
signature; the corresponding private key remains with the author. Do not
confuse these keys with SSH login keys, HTTPS server trust, or SNMP
credentials.


Accept a signing identity


When importing an unfamiliar package, compare the signing key with
independently obtained author information before accepting it. A package's
embedded author name and homepage are claims made by that package, not
independent verification. Cacti validates the signature and enforces the
implementation's key requirements; a signature alone does not prove that
included code is appropriate for your installation.


Acceptance stores the public identity for later imports. Review the accepted
keys when an author rotates keys or an identity is no longer trusted. A valid
signature made with a different key is a different signing identity even if the
displayed author name is unchanged.


Remove or replace trust


Removing an accepted key changes the stored trust decision for future imports.
It does not undo templates already imported, remove installed scripts, or
revoke an author's access to another system. Cacti also recognizes its
configured built-in signing identities; deleting an accepted-key row does not
redefine those built-in identities.


If a package is invalid or fails the current key requirements, obtain a
corrected release from its author. Do not weaken validation or edit the signed
data to make an import pass.


Publish packages


Authors generate signing material with cli/genkey.php; see Create
Packages <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Create-Packages.md>. Keep package.key private and protect the
configured PKI directory from HTTP access. Preserve a secured backup so updates
can retain the same identity. Use key replacement deliberately and tell
recipients how to verify the new public identity independently.


This screen manages package trust on the development branch. It is not a
configuration step for Cacti 1.2's ordinary XML importer. See Import
Packages <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Import-Packages.md> and Template Import <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Import-Template.md> for the
different formats and workflows.




Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


Apache Template Config


The Apache template collects server statistics over HTTP from
/server-status?auto, using Apache's mod_status module. These examples allow
local access. Check the mod_status documentation <https://httpd.apache.org/docs/2.4/mod/mod_status.html> for the available
statistics and use the endpoint on the server being monitored.


[Image: Apache template view] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/apache-template-preview.png>


Enterprise Linux configuration


Check whether the installed Apache configuration already loads mod_status.
Add the module load only if it is missing, then configure the status location
in the site's Apache configuration, for example under /etc/httpd/conf.d:


LoadModule status_module modules/mod_status.so
ExtendedStatus On

<Location /server-status>
    SetHandler server-status
    Require local
</Location>



With administrator privileges, test the configuration before reloading a running
service:


apachectl configtest && systemctl reload httpd



Debian configuration


Create /etc/apache2/conf-available/80-server-status.conf with:


ExtendedStatus On

<Location /server-status>
    SetHandler server-status
    Require local
</Location>



Enable the module and configuration with administrator privileges. Reload only
if the configuration test succeeds:


a2enmod status
a2enconf 80-server-status
apache2ctl configtest && systemctl reload apache2



Verify the collection endpoint


Request http://127.0.0.1/server-status?auto locally and check for the expected
machine-readable statistics. Then verify collection through Cacti under the
account and from the host that actually runs the helper.


Require local rejects a collector on a different host. For remote collection,
configure access for the intended collector addresses and network path rather
than making server-status public. The package does not configure the target's
Apache service or access controls for you.




Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


ESXi/VMware Template


The bundled ESXi template polls the ESXi host through SNMP. Configure the
host's SNMP agent and allow the Cacti collector to reach its polling port. Run
ESXCLI as an authorized administrator through the ESXi Shell or SSH.


For an SNMPv2c configuration, inspect the existing settings first:


esxcli system snmp get



The following example uses UDP port 161. Replace REPLACE_WITH_READ_COMMUNITY
with your chosen community before running it. The --communities option
replaces the existing community list; preserve other required communities when
changing an existing configuration.


esxcli system snmp set --port 161
esxcli system snmp set --communities 'REPLACE_WITH_READ_COMMUNITY'
esxcli system snmp set --enable true



These options are documented in Broadcom's ESXCLI command reference <https://developer.broadcom.com/xapis/esxcli-command-reference/latest/namespace/esxcli_system.html>.
They configure the agent; they do not prove that routing or firewall rules
permit polling. Allow the intended collector to reach the host's SNMP port
using the firewall procedure for your ESXi release.


Check the resulting configuration:


esxcli system snmp get



In Cacti's device settings <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Devices.md>, select the ESXi template and matching
SNMP version, community and port. Verify the returned queries and graphs from
the assigned collector. Use your ESXi release's SNMPv3 configuration procedure
if required; the community example above does not configure SNMPv3.




Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


Proxmox - external storage usage


The Linux template is used for Proxmox, it allows you to create disk usage graphs.
However, this is not always possible with external storage.


You can check the usage of volumes on external storage with the pvesm status command.


Here are instructions on how to get this value into SNMP and use it in Cacti.


We will work with volumes named alletra-lvm and local


pvesm status
Name               Type     Status           Total            Used       Available        %
alletra-lvm         lvm     active     10737401856      1927315456      8810086400   17.95%
local               dir     active        98497780        23395264        70052968   23.75%



apt-get install sudo



Run visudo and add line


Debian-snmp ALL=(ALL) NOPASSWD: /usr/sbin/pvesm status



Create a systemd drop-in file to allow snmpd to call the monitoring script.
Use a drop-in rather than editing the service file directly, so package
updates do not overwrite your changes:


mkdir -p /etc/systemd/system/snmpd.service.d
cat > /etc/systemd/system/snmpd.service.d/proxmox-monitoring.conf << 'EOF'
[Service]
# Allow snmpd to execute the monitoring script and call sudo pvesm.
# PrivateTmp is disabled because some Proxmox storage backends access
# /tmp during status queries. Narrow this further if your storage
# backends do not require /tmp access.
PrivateTmp=false
EOF
systemctl daemon-reload






Note: Avoid setting ProtectSystem=off or ProtectHome=off unless
you have confirmed that pvesm requires broader filesystem access on your
specific Proxmox version. The sudoers entry above already restricts
the script to a single command, which limits the blast radius of a
compromised snmpd process.



Create new shell script /usr/local/bin/monitoring.sh:


#!/bin/bash

STORAGE="$1"

/usr/bin/sudo /usr/sbin/pvesm status | /usr/bin/awk -v lv="$STORAGE" '$1==lv {gsub("%","",$NF); print $NF}'



Make it executable:
chmod 755 /usr/local/bin/monitoring.sh


Add these lines to /etc/snmp/snmpd.conf. Alletra-lvm and local are from pvesm status:


extend-sh alletra-lvm /usr/local/bin/monitoring.sh alletra-lvm
extend-sh local /usr/local/bin/monitoring.sh local



Restart snmpd:


systemctl daemon-reload
systemctl restart snmpd



Now we can check if data are in snmp:


snmpwalk -v2c -c your_community -On  localhost .1.3.6.1.4.1.8072.1.3
.1.3.6.1.4.1.8072.1.3.2.1.0 = INTEGER: 2
.1.3.6.1.4.1.8072.1.3.2.2.1.2.5.108.111.99.97.108 = STRING: "/usr/local/bin/monitoring.sh"
.1.3.6.1.4.1.8072.1.3.2.2.1.2.11.97.108.108.101.116.114.97.45.108.118.109 = STRING: "/usr/local/bin/monitoring.sh"
.1.3.6.1.4.1.8072.1.3.2.2.1.3.5.108.111.99.97.108 = STRING: "local"
.1.3.6.1.4.1.8072.1.3.2.2.1.3.11.97.108.108.101.116.114.97.45.108.118.109 = STRING: "alletra-lvm"
.1.3.6.1.4.1.8072.1.3.2.2.1.4.5.108.111.99.97.108 = ""
.1.3.6.1.4.1.8072.1.3.2.2.1.4.11.97.108.108.101.116.114.97.45.108.118.109 = ""

.1.3.6.1.4.1.8072.1.3.2.2.1.6.5.108.111.99.97.108 = INTEGER: 2
.1.3.6.1.4.1.8072.1.3.2.2.1.6.11.97.108.108.101.116.114.97.45.108.118.109 = INTEGER: 2
.1.3.6.1.4.1.8072.1.3.2.2.1.7.5.108.111.99.97.108 = INTEGER: 1

.1.3.6.1.4.1.8072.1.3.2.2.1.20.5.108.111.99.97.108 = INTEGER: 4
.1.3.6.1.4.1.8072.1.3.2.2.1.20.11.97.108.108.101.116.114.97.45.108.118.109 = INTEGER: 4
.1.3.6.1.4.1.8072.1.3.2.2.1.21.5.108.111.99.97.108 = INTEGER: 1
.1.3.6.1.4.1.8072.1.3.2.2.1.21.11.97.108.108.101.116.114.97.45.108.118.109 = INTEGER: 1
.1.3.6.1.4.1.8072.1.3.2.3.1.1.5.108.111.99.97.108 = STRING: "23.75"
.1.3.6.1.4.1.8072.1.3.2.3.1.1.11.97.108.108.101.116.114.97.45.108.118.109 = STRING: "17.95"
.1.3.6.1.4.1.8072.1.3.2.3.1.2.5.108.111.99.97.108 = STRING: "23.75"

These OIDs contain requested information. OID depends on names used in snmpd.conf:


.1.3.6.1.4.1.8072.1.3.2.3.1.1.5.108.111.99.97.108 = STRING: "23.75"
.1.3.6.1.4.1.8072.1.3.2.3.1.1.11.97.108.108.101.116.114.97.45.108.118.109 = STRING: "17.95"



We can graph these OIDs with SNMP - generic OID template (Graph-a-Single-SNMP-OID.md)

Data Source Debugging


If you are having issues with blank graphs and have done the basic checks such
as permissions the Data source debug tool can help troubleshoot what
information is coming into Cacti.


The debug tool will check the data source, graphs and permissions along incoming
data and graph settings, such as step count, to ensure the definition matches
the profile of the data source amongst other things.


The tool allows you to run a debug on one or many data source. To request debug
information, tick all applicable data sources you require information on, then
select 'Run Check' in the actions drop down list.  This will put all of the
selected data sources into debug mode.


For a single data source, you can click the data source name which will also
put it into debug mode.


[Image: Data Source Debug] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/data-debug.png>


You can see the status of each data source along with all of the checks run
against it. Successful checks show a green checkmark, whilst failed checks shows
a red X.  Depending on the issue a recommendation will be provided for a failed
check.


[Image: Data Source Debug Select Data Sources] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/data-debug1.png>


[Image: Data Debug Tests Complete] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/data-debug3.png>


A summary of the checks as well as the properties of the data source
can be displayed by clicking on the data source name.  This is useful
for a single page view of the data source and graph details.


[Image: Data Debug Test Details] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/data-debug4.png>


This page also shows what the last values where when last polled. This is also
useful for troubleshooting as if the data comes in out of range of your Data
Source template, or Graph template, this could point you into the right direction.


If the Web Server has write access to Cacti's RRA folder, if the Data Debug finds
and issue that it knows how to correct.  It will offer you a link to make that
correction.





NOTE: Devices that are down for long periods of time will report as having errors
since the RRDfiles associated with them will not be updated as expected for with an
available Device.





Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


The Cacti Log Interface


Summary


The Cacti Log is where all Cacti statistics, warnings, errors and other log
messages are sent. Cacti can optionally log some information to your syslog or
event log
depending on your OS type.


The Cacti Log is by default ordered from newest event to oldest event, and
log rotation follows Settings > Paths or the operating-system package
configuration. Cacti can rotate logs daily, weekly, or monthly when its own
rotation is enabled.


If you have the User Level permission, you can view any available Cacti Standard
Output or Standard Error log. If you have the Administrative level access,
you may additionally purge information from the logs.


As a Cacti Administrator, you have the ability to filter out some message types
from users for example, one message type that people like to filter out is
"AUTH" messages that tell you who is logging into the Cacti Web Site.
You can edit these removal rules from the Cacti Settings page.


An image of the Cacti Log file can be seen below.


[Image: Cacti Log File] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/cacti-log.png>


Cacti Log Filters


What we like to see in the Cacti Log is a lot of Green, which means good. By
default,
WARNINGS are yellow, and ERRORS are red. But there are other types of messages
that highlight differently. Generally, you can filter for these message types.
The filters include:


* File - The Cacti Log file to view. Rotated files are suffixed with the
date they were rotated, using the pattern YYYY-MM-DD.

* Tail Lines - The number of lines to view from the selected Cacti Log.

* Types - The message types to view. These will be explained below.

* Display Order - The order to display the Log lines, either Newest First
or Oldest First.

* Refresh - How often to Auto-refresh the interface.

* Search - A Perl compatible regular expression to search the log with.



The Cacti Log Types include the following:


* Stats - Anything that includes the word STATS


* Warnings - Anything that includes the word WARNING


* Warnings++ - Anything of each WARNING, ERROR, DEBUG, and
SQL. This one is good for debugging, and it includes the errors that
matter most to the Cacti Administrator.


* Errors - Anything that includes the word ERROR


* Errors++ - Anything of each ERROR, DEBUG, and SQL


* Debug - Anything that includes the word DEBUG


* SQL Calls - Anything that includes the word SQL


* AutoM8 - Anything that includes the word AUTOM8. These messages come
from Cacti automation of Devices, Graphs, and Trees


* Non Stats - Anything that does not include the word STATS.


* Boost - Anything that contains the word BOOST.


* Device Up/Down - Any device Up/Down notification, including those that
come from the Thold or Monitor plugins.


* Recaches - Any Device Recache Events.


* Security Issues - Added in Cacti 1.3. Anything logged by Cacti's
security auditing.


* Threshold - If the Thold plugin is installed, any of its threshold
breach and restoration events.




Differences between User and Administrative Views


As previously mentioned, the Cacti log has two permission levels,
the User Permission level will not include the Purge button,
and can have some information hidden from public view
whereas the Administrative view includes all Log messages and a Purge button.




Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


Debugging


Use this procedure when graphs show gaps, unknown values (NaN), or unexpected
zeros. Start with one affected device and data source, and identify the failing
stage: device or script, collector, database buffering, RRD storage, or graph
rendering. The commands and menu labels follow Cacti development (1.3).


Use Data Source Debugging <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Data-Debug.md> for the guided checks. Record the
device, data-source and collector IDs, the expected polling interval, and the
last successful sample. A down device or an idle counter can legitimately
produce a different result from a failed collector.


Check Cacti Log File


Read the configured Cacti log <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Cacti-Log.md>, including the affected remote
collector's log. The log path can be outside the application directory; the
FreeBSD package normally uses /var/log/cacti.


Look for timeouts, authentication failures, script errors, database errors and
poller overruns around the missing samples. Preserve the original log. Enable
additional logging only for the affected device where supported, or briefly
for a controlled polling cycle, then restore the previous logging level.
Redact credentials and sensitive device details before sharing diagnostics.


An SNMP timeout does not establish that bulk requests are the cause. Check
reachability, SNMP version, credentials, access controls and response time
first. If the agent fails larger requests, adjust that device's request limits
and retest rather than lowering global limits for every device.


Check Basic Data Gathering


Run a data-input script as the actual poller account, using the configured
interpreter, arguments and environment. Compare its output with the expected
field names and numeric values in the [Data Input Method](Data-Input-
Methods.md).
For a script-server input, use the PHP Script Server <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/PHP-Script-Server.md>
procedure; executing a function-based script directly is not an equivalent
test.


For SNMP, query the exact OID with the same version, context and access
settings
as Cacti. Configure secrets through the supported protected Net-SNMP
configuration rather than pasting real credentials into shared commands or
logs. Check both the device response and Cacti's configured timeout/retries.
A successful ping alone does not confirm that SNMP is available.


Check Cacti's poller


Verify the responsible Data Collector <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Data-Collectors.md>, its selected
polling engine, and Data Collection Enabled. Check the actual launcher and
Cron/Daemon Interval as well as Poller Interval. FreeBSD uses cron or
configured rc.d services; Linux systemd commands do not apply there.


If a manual collector test is needed, first pause the overlapping scheduled
work and wait for existing workers to finish. Restrict the test to the affected
device. The development PHP collector uses named ID arguments, as shown by its
command help <https://github.com/Cacti/cacti/blob/ff31e051d/cmd.php#L1372-L1391>:


php -q cmd.php --first=42 --last=42 --debug



Replace 42 with the device ID and run from the Cacti directory as the poller
account. This collects data; it is not a read-only inspection. Restore the
original schedule after the test. For Spine, use the installed binary's
--help and Spine <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Spine.md> instructions for its version and configuration.


Check MySQL updating


Check the collector log for failed database writes, connection errors or
replication problems. Inspect the Poller Cache <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/System-Utilities.md> and
confirm that the affected data source is assigned to the expected collector.


Cacti can buffer samples for later processing. With Boost <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Boost.md> or an
RRD cache enabled, collection and disk updates can occur at different times.
Check the relevant worker and pending-data state before concluding that a
missing direct rrdtool update log entry means collection failed.


Do not replay captured SQL writes against the production database as a debug
step; they can duplicate or overwrite application state.


Check RRDfile updating


Use the RRD path shown by the data source and its configured collector. Do not
assume it is under the web application's rra directory. On a default FreeBSD
package installation, RRDs are under /var/db/cacti/rra.


Check the file's timestamps, data-source names and last update:


rrdtool info /path/to/device.rrd
rrdtool lastupdate /path/to/device.rrd



Use the configured RRDtool binary and, when applicable, the configured RRD
cache
connection. Account for pending cache writes before comparing disk timestamps.
Do not issue manual production rrdtool update commands to fill a gap.


Check RRDfile ownership


The poller or RRD-writing service must be able to traverse the parent
directories
and write the RRD file. The graph-rendering process needs the access required
by the configured storage/cache arrangement. Check those actual service
identities rather than assuming they are the same user.


Inspect the affected file and its parent directories. Correct the specific
ownership or permissions that differ from the deployment's policy. Avoid
blanket ownership changes to application code, credentials, or every RRD file.
See the FreeBSD installation guidance <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/General-Installing-Instructions.md#freebsd> for package defaults.


Check RRDfile numbers


Read recent stored values and metadata:


rrdtool fetch /path/to/device.rrd AVERAGE --start -1h
rrdtool info /path/to/device.rrd



Check data-source type, minimum, maximum, step and heartbeat against the
Data Source Template <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Data-Source-Templates.md> and
Data Profile <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Data-Profiles.md>. An out-of-range value, counter reset or gap
beyond the heartbeat can produce unknown data even when collection succeeds.


Changing a template does not necessarily rewrite existing RRD definitions.
Plan any RRD repair or tuning against the actual files, preserve a consistent
backup, and verify the result before applying it to other data sources.
Increasing heartbeat can permit interpolation; it does not recover observed
measurements that were never collected.


Check rrdtool graph statement


In Graph Management, open the affected graph and enable Graph Debug Mode.
Inspect the command and RRDtool response. Check the DEF paths, data-source
names, consolidation functions, requested time range and graph permissions.


If reproducing the command as the web account, preserve its binary paths and
environment. Replace a graph - or graphv - output destination with a
writable
temporary image path so binary image output does not obscure diagnostics.


Duplicate Poller Entries


Inspect Cacti-specific cron entries, service configuration and running workers
on the responsible collector. A package may have supplied a schedule in
addition to one added manually. Keep one intended launcher; do not disable the
system-wide cron service to stop a single Cacti job.


After resolving duplicate launches, verify a complete polling cycle and fresh
RRD samples. Preserve the original service and scheduling configuration for
rollback. See Settings - Poller <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Settings-Poller.md>.


Not NaN, but 0 (zero) values


Distinguish a valid zero from a script failure converted to zero. Run the input
under the poller account, check its exit status and output, and verify external
command paths. A successful interactive command as root does not establish
that the collector has the same permissions or environment.


For tools that require elevated access, use a narrowly scoped mechanism
supported by that tool or its package. Do not make a script or general-purpose
interpreter setuid as a troubleshooting shortcut.




Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


Frequently Asked Questions


These answers follow Cacti's develop branch, the unreleased 1.3 series.
Menu labels and available options can differ in released versions. For Cacti
1.2, use the 1.2.x manual <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/tree/1.2.x> and your installed version's code.


Using Cacti


Q: How do I create traffic graphs?


A: First, configure read access to the device's SNMP interface counters.
Cacti's PHP SNMP functions use the PHP SNMP extension where supported and
fall back to Net-SNMP command-line tools for other requests. Configure the
installed binaries under Console > Configuration > Settings > Paths.
Installing the PHP extension does not eliminate every need for these tools.
Spine uses its own Net-SNMP support for polling.


* Open Console > Management > Devices and add a device.


* Enter a description and hostname or IP address, select a suitable Device
Template, and configure the SNMP version and credentials. For SNMPv3,
match the device's username, authentication, privacy, and context settings.


* Save the device and check the SNMP information displayed on its edit page.
Ensure SNMP - Interface Statistics is in Associated Data Queries.
Add the query if the selected template did not supply it. Use Verbose
Query if it returns no interfaces.


* Select Create New Graphs, then select the interfaces in the interface
statistics query and choose the desired graph type. Use a 64-bit counter
graph where the device supports it, especially for high-speed interfaces.


* Click Create and complete any additional fields presented. Allow
polling to collect samples before expecting traffic data.




See How to Graph Your Network <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/How-to-Graph-Your-Network.md> and
Data Queries <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Data-Queries.md> for the full workflow.


General


Q: The website displays PHP source instead of a login page. What is wrong?


A: The web server is serving PHP files without passing them to PHP.
Configure its PHP handler, such as PHP-FPM or the Apache PHP module used by
your installation. Having a working php command in a terminal does not
confirm that the web server can execute PHP.


Keep the site inaccessible to other users until this is fixed: source files
can contain database credentials. Check the web server and PHP service logs,
then follow the installation guide <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/General-Installing-Instructions.md> for
your operating system and web server.




Q: I have forgotten my local admin password. How do I reset it?


A: If another administrator can log in, use
User Management <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/User-Management.md> to reset the local account. Otherwise,
connect to the Cacti database with an account permitted to update user_auth:


mariadb -u root -p cacti



Replace cacti with your database name and newpassword with a unique
temporary password, escaping any SQL quote characters in the value:


UPDATE user_auth
SET password = SHA2('newpassword', 256),
    password_change = 'on',
    must_change_password = 'on',
    lastchange = UNIX_TIMESTAMP()
WHERE username = 'admin' AND realm = 0;



The MySQL mysql client accepts the same connection arguments. Cacti accepts
this SHA-256 bootstrap hash and re-hashes it with its current password
algorithm after successful local authentication. The account must then change
the temporary password. The reset timestamp also preserves password-expiration
behavior. See the local login provider <https://github.com/Cacti/cacti/blob/883b334de/lib/Auth/LocalAuthLoginProvider.php> and
password verification <https://github.com/Cacti/cacti/blob/883b334de/lib/auth.php#L4550-L4566> implementations.


Changing the password does not unlock or enable an account. If login still
fails, check its status in User Management or inspect it in the database:


SELECT username, realm, enabled, locked, failed_attempts, lastfail
FROM user_auth
WHERE username = 'admin' AND realm = 0;



Have an administrator resolve an unintended lockout or disabled status. For
LDAP or another external login provider, reset the password at that provider.


Monitoring


Q: Polling does not finish within the configured interval. What can I do?


A: Check the Cacti log for slow or unreachable devices, script timeouts,
database errors, and runtime warnings. Compare poller statistics with the
configured interval before changing concurrency.


Spine <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Spine.md> is the multithreaded C alternative to the PHP cmd.php
collector. poller.php still launches the selected collector and coordinates
the polling cycle. Install Spine, configure its database access and paths,
then select it under Settings > Poller > Poller Type.


Tune Processes and Spine Threads for the affected entry under
Data Collectors. Threads are per collector process, so consider the total
concurrency and database connections. Increase gradually while measuring
runtime, CPU, memory, database load, and storage I/O. More threads do not
guarantee faster polling; there is no fixed item throughput or CPU ratio that
applies to every installation. See Data Collectors <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Data-Collectors.md>.




Q: I changed a setting or template and some graphs stopped updating.


A: Use Data Debug <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Data-Debug.md> and inspect the affected device's
View Poller Cache entries. Confirm that the data sources still have valid
polling commands, credentials, and OIDs.


If entries are missing or stale, use Repopulate Poller Cache on the device
edit page or save that device again. For a targeted CLI rebuild, run this from
the Cacti directory as the polling account, replacing 123 with the device ID:


php -q cli/rebuild_poller_cache.php --host-id=123



For a system-wide rebuild, use Utilities > System Utilities > Rebuild Poller
Cache, or omit --host-id from the CLI command. Large installations should
use the CLI. Rebuilding the cache does not repair a faulty template or restore
lost RRD data. Back up the database, RRD files, and custom scripts and query XML
before changing templates. See the cache rebuild utility <https://github.com/Cacti/cacti/blob/883b334de/cli/rebuild_poller_cache.php>.




Q: SNMP queries fail. Where should I start?


A: Test from the Data Collector assigned to the device, using the same
SNMP version, credentials, port, and context as Cacti. A successful query from
your workstation does not prove that the collector can reach the agent.


Check the agent's listening address, firewall rules, access controls, and
supported authentication/privacy protocols. For Cacti's PHP paths, verify the
PHP SNMP extension and the Net-SNMP binary paths. For Spine, also inspect its
configuration and log messages. Use the device's Verbose Query output to
distinguish an inaccessible agent from an empty or unsupported data query.




Q: How do I configure Net-SNMP's agent for Cacti?


A: Configure snmpd on the monitored system. snmpconf -g basic_setup
can help generate a configuration; confirm which file the installed service
actually reads. Paths differ between operating systems and packages.


Use read-only access and restrict requests to your Data Collectors. Prefer
SNMPv3 authentication and privacy where supported. If you use SNMPv2c, this
example restricts a community to one collector:


# Replace both placeholders with your own values.
rocommunity REPLACE_WITH_UNIQUE_COMMUNITY 192.0.2.10



Replace the example IP with the collector's source address as seen by the
agent. Set matching credentials in Cacti and permit the configured SNMP UDP
port through the firewall only from the collectors. Apply the configuration
using your platform's service manager. On a systemd installation whose service
is named snmpd, for example:


sudo systemctl restart snmpd



Use the Net-SNMP configuration manual <https://www.net-snmp.org/docs/man/snmpd.conf.html> for SNMPv3 user
creation, views, and any disk-monitoring options required by your templates.




Q: I scheduled network discovery, but nothing is happening.


A: Open the Log tab and look for recent SYSTEM polling statistics,
AUTOM8 messages, and errors. Missing statistics can indicate a stopped
poller, an early failure, or logging settings that suppress or redirect the
messages. Check the active log destination and filters before concluding
that polling has stopped.


Confirm Settings > Poller > Data Collection Enabled is checked. Verify
that your chosen scheduler, such as cron or cactid, is running under the
intended account and can execute PHP and access Cacti. Check its service or
scheduler logs for startup and permission errors. Use one scheduler for each
collector to avoid duplicate launches.


If polling works, inspect the enabled state, schedule, assigned collector,
address range, and SNMP options of the network under Automation > Networks.
Discovery is launched from the polling cycle and has its own scheduling and
process checks. See Automation Networks <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Automation-Networks.md>,
Discovered Devices <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Discovered-Devices.md>, and the
automation implementation <https://github.com/Cacti/cacti/blob/883b334de/poller_automation.php>.


Graphs


Q: I just installed Cacti and my graphs appear as broken images.


A: A graph cannot read its data until the corresponding RRD files exist.
Confirm that polling has run successfully and inspect the Cacti log for RRD
creation errors. The normal local storage directory is rra/, but use the
actual data source path if you configured another storage layout or RRDproxy.


The polling account needs access to create and update those files. The web
server needs access to render graphs and, when it performs RRD updates or
maintenance, write access as well. See
installation hardening <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Hardening-Cacti-Installation.md> for permissions.


To diagnose a polling failure, run from the Cacti directory as the configured
polling account, while avoiding an overlapping scheduled run:


php -q poller.php --debug



Use the PHP CLI binary configured for your installation. If the files exist,
open the graph under Management > Graphs, select Turn On Graph Debug
Mode, and inspect the RRDtool command and response. Check the configured
RRDtool path and the web server's access to the files named in that command.




Q: My graphs render, but they do not contain data.


A: Start with Data Debug <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Data-Debug.md> to confirm that polling returns
valid values. Inspect the log for SNMP, script, and RRD update errors. Check
that the polling account can update the actual RRD path. A manual run as
another user can leave files with unsuitable ownership or permissions.


Check the data source type, minimum, maximum, heartbeat, and polling interval.
Values outside the RRD data source bounds become Unknown. For counters, the
bounds apply to the calculated rate, so use the data source's units when
choosing a maximum. A traffic graph may convert byte rates to bits for display.
Counter-based sources also need successive samples before a rate is available.
See Data Sources <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Data-Sources.md> and Debugging <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Debugging.md>.




Q: Long ifAlias values are truncated in graph titles. Can I change this?


A: Under Settings > Visual > Graph/Data Source/Data Query Settings,
increase Data Source Field Length (max_data_query_field_length). Its
development-branch default is 40, not 15. Also check Maximum Title
Length, whose default is 110.


If existing names still contain the shortened text, use Reapply Suggested
Names for the affected graphs or data sources. This regenerates names from
their naming rules, so check custom names before applying it in bulk. See
setting definitions <https://github.com/Cacti/cacti/blob/883b334de/include/global_settings.php#L1404-L1421> and query substitution <https://github.com/Cacti/cacti/blob/883b334de/lib/variables.php>.




Q: A device rebooted and now there is a large spike on its traffic graph.


A: Counter resets or discontinuities can produce an implausible rate.
Check the counter type, interface speed, and the maximum for the RRD data
source. A realistic maximum lets RRDtool reject impossible calculated rates.
Use the source's units, such as bytes per second, rather than the graph's
displayed units.


For an existing spike, see Spikekill <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Spikekill.md>. Back up the affected RRD
files before removing spikes because this changes stored history.




Q: A remote collector times out even though SNMP responses are fast.


A: SNMP is only one part of a remote collection cycle. Check the logs on
both collectors and verify connectivity, latency, and database access between
the remote collector and the main system. Also inspect replication/recovery
messages, database load, script runtimes, and RRD processing.


The Remote Agent Timeout setting controls how long the central web server
waits for remote device-information requests. It is not the polling cycle's
runtime limit. Diagnose the timed-out operation before changing it. See
Remote Poller Setup <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/How-To-Setup-Remote-Pollers.md>.




Q: RRDtool reports an unknown option or a malformed COMMENT. What next?


A: Check Settings > Paths > RRDtool Binary Path and
Settings > General > RRDtool Version against the executable actually used
by Cacti. A version mismatch can make Cacti generate unsupported options.


Enable Graph Debug Mode to inspect the failing command. A malformed COMMENT
can also come from graph text or escaping; a version mismatch is not the only
possible cause. Check the exact option or graph item named in the error.


Windows Related


Q: PHP reports Cannot redeclare title_trim(). How do I fix it?


A: This means PHP loaded more than one definition of the function. Check
for a mixed installation, duplicate Cacti files, and custom PHP include or
auto_prepend_file settings that load Cacti code twice. Check the PHP
configuration used by the failing web or CLI process, since they can differ.


Do not add a Cacti directory to PHP's include_path as an installation step.
Use a complete, consistent release and follow the
Windows installation guide <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Installing-Under-Windows.md>. The error alone
does not establish that include_path is the cause.


Common Problems


MySQL server has gone away


This means the database connection was lost. Check the database error log and
service status for restarts or crashes, then investigate network failures,
connection timeouts, and oversized packets. Inspect relevant settings:


SHOW GLOBAL VARIABLES LIKE 'wait_timeout';
SHOW GLOBAL VARIABLES LIKE 'max_allowed_packet';
SHOW GLOBAL VARIABLES LIKE 'max_connections';
SHOW GLOBAL STATUS LIKE 'Threads_connected';
SHOW GLOBAL STATUS LIKE 'Max_used_connections';



Connection exhaustion normally reports Too many connections. Raise
max_connections only when the logs and workload support that diagnosis and
the server has capacity. Increasing it alone does not fix a lost connection.
See the MySQL troubleshooting guide <https://dev.mysql.com/doc/refman/8.4/en/gone-away.html>.


Cron is out of sync with the Poller Interval


A scheduling warning can indicate that the launcher cadence and Cacti's
configuration disagree, or that launches were delayed or missed. Under
Console > Configuration > Settings > Poller, check Cron/Daemon Interval
and Poller Interval.


Cron/Daemon Interval is the launch frequency, with choices of 60 or 300
seconds. Poller Interval is the collection frequency; poller.php can
perform multiple collection passes during a single launch. Changing the
poller interval also requires rebuilding the poller cache; existing RRD
profiles need separate consideration. See the
interval-change guide <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/How-To-Poller-5-to-1-min.md>.


For cron, the schedule must match Cron/Daemon Interval. For example, these
are alternative entries in a system cron file. Replace the account and paths
with your installation's values:


# Every five minutes (300 seconds)
*/5 * * * * cacti /usr/bin/php -q /path/to/cacti/poller.php

# Every minute (60 seconds)
* * * * * cacti /usr/bin/php -q /path/to/cacti/poller.php



Install only the matching entry. A per-user crontab omits the cacti account
field. If using cactid, check its service configuration and disable the
equivalent cron entry. Also check for runtime overruns and scheduler errors.
See the daemon installation instructions <https://github.com/Cacti/cacti/blob/883b334de/service/README.md>.


Poller Runtime exceeded


Maximum runtime of ... seconds exceeded. Exiting.



This means the poller reached its runtime limit. Check earlier log messages
and collector statistics to identify the slow or failing work:


* Spine cannot connect with its configured database credentials or cannot
start from the configured binary/configuration paths.

* Unreachable devices, slow SNMP requests, or scripts consume the interval.

* Collector processes or Spine threads are insufficient for the workload,
or excessive concurrency overloads the database or system.

* Database connection limits, CPU, memory, storage I/O, or remote connectivity
constrain the cycle.



Resolve the measured bottleneck, then confirm subsequent cycles finish within
the configured interval. See poller orchestration <https://github.com/Cacti/cacti/blob/883b334de/poller.php>.


RRDtool reports Unknown Error


Edit the affected graph and select Turn On Graph Debug Mode. Inspect both
RRDtool Command and RRDtool Says. If necessary, run the displayed
command as the web server account, using the same executable, paths, and
environment. On Linux, an administrator can use sudo -u to select that
account.


Graph commands can write binary image data to standard output. When copying
one that uses rrdtool graph - or graphv -, change the output destination
from - to a writable temporary image path so diagnostics remain readable.


On Windows, reproduce the failure under the relevant application-pool or
scheduled-task identity. These accounts can have different permissions, PHP
configurations, and environment variables; an interactive administrator's
successful command does not prove that the service account can run it.


Source References


Cacti behavior in this page was checked against development commit
883b334de <https://github.com/Cacti/cacti/tree/883b334de>. The links below pin the relevant implementation
so that setting names, defaults, and authentication behavior can be checked
when updating this FAQ. Also see SNMP method selection <https://github.com/Cacti/cacti/blob/883b334de/lib/snmp.php>,
device actions <https://github.com/Cacti/cacti/blob/883b334de/host.php>, collector concurrency <https://github.com/Cacti/cacti/blob/883b334de/pollers.php>, and
RRD creation and graph generation <https://github.com/Cacti/cacti/blob/883b334de/lib/rrd.php>.

Known Issues


Use the issue tracker and release notes to check whether a problem affects
your installed version. This page points to those maintained sources;
it is not a complete list of open defects.


Check existing reports


* Open Cacti issues <https://github.com/Cacti/cacti/issues?q=is%3Aissue+is%3Aopen>
include reported problems and proposed improvements. An open report is not
confirmation that every release is affected.

* Cacti releases <https://github.com/Cacti/cacti/releases> describe published
versions. Check the changelog included with your version and the target
release of any linked fix. A fix merged into develop may not be available
in a released maintenance version.

* Security advisories <https://github.com/Cacti/cacti/security/advisories>
describe published vulnerabilities, affected versions, and available fixes.

* For a plugin problem, use the issue tracker and release notes of that
Cacti plugin's repository <https://github.com/orgs/Cacti/repositories>.



Search both open and closed issues using the error message, affected feature,
and version. Before applying a workaround, confirm that it matches your
release and environment. Back up the database, RRD files, and configuration
before an upgrade or a change that can alter stored data.


Troubleshoot or report a problem


For installation and usage questions, search the
Cacti Forums <https://forums.cacti.net/>. For missing measurements or blank
graphs, start with data source debugging <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Data-Debug.md> and the
Cacti log <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Cacti-Log.md>.


If you find a reproducible defect, follow the
contribution guide <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Contributing.md>. Include the Cacti and Spine versions,
operating system, PHP and database versions, steps to reproduce, expected
behavior, and relevant logs with credentials and sensitive information removed.


Report suspected vulnerabilities privately using the
Cacti security policy <https://github.com/Cacti/cacti/security/policy>.
Do not put credentials or exploit details in a public issue.

Spikekill


Warning


Spikekill is designed to overwrite specific data points in one or more RRD
files. You should use great caution when running Spikekill, and you should
always ensure you have proper backups from which to restore your RRDfiles if
Spikekill performs a modification that you did not expect.


Overview


Spikekill is a tool used to remove spikes in a graph.  Spikes can appear in a
graph after a device reboots, or when you switch from 32-bit to 64-bit interface
counters on a device.  Spikekill works by statistically analyzing the data
contained inside an RRDfile, and overwriting specific data points.  It offers
4 methods by which to analyze the data and selectively overwrite data points:


* Standard Deviation


Calculates the standard
deviation <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_deviation> of the data, and
overwrites data points that are N times higher than the standard deviation.


* Variance Average


Calculates the average value of the data, and overwrites data points that are
N percent higher than the average.


* GapFill


Finds gaps in the data (missing data points) and also finds data points that
are N percent higher than the average, and overwrites them, but only does
so inside the specified time range.


* Float


Overwrites all data points that are within a specified time range.




How to use


GUI


Spikekill is easily run on a graph with a couple mouse clicks in the Cacti GUI:


* On Cacti's main Graph tab, click the Spikekill icon
[Image: Spikekill-icon] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/spikekill.gif> next to a graph.


* In the drop-down menu that appears, review your current settings by hovering
on Settings and reviewing each item, making changes if desired.


* Run Spikekill by choosing one of the four methods available in the drop-down
menu. Spikekill runs immediately, and the graph may be modified, depending on
your settings.  The graph is refreshed when Spikekill is finished.




CLI


Spikekill also has a flexible command line interface.  The following example
will help you get started.


shell>php cli/removespikes.php --help



Settings


Spikekill requires certain values to successfully calculate which data points to
overwrite.  The following settings are customizable:


* Replacement Method


When Spikekill identifies data points to overwrite, the
value of Replacement Method represents the data that will be written in
place of each data point.


When Replacement Method is set to NaN, running Spikekill using GapFill
or Float methods will perform no modifications. Please be aware of Issue
#3673 <https://github.com/Cacti/cacti/issues/3673>, where 'Last Known Good'
is always used, regardless of setting.


* Standard Deviations


When using the Standard Deviation method, the value of Standard Deviations
is the coefficient which determines how many times above the standard
deviation the data must be to be considered a spike.


* Variance Percentage


When using the Variance Average method, the value of Variance Percentage is
the coefficient which determines how much higher than the average the data
must be to be considered a spike.


* Variance Outliers


When using the Variance Average method, the highest N values and the lowest
N values are considered outliers.  These outliers are ignored when
calculating the average of the data.


* Kills Per RRA


Spikekill will limit the number of data points that it
overwrites to the value of Kills Per RRA.  For reference, a single RRDfile
can contain multiple data sources and multiple archives.  Therefore, the
total number of data points that could be overwritten in a single RRDfile
will be determined by the formula:


(number_of_RRAs) x (number_of_data_sources) x (Kills_Per_RRA)



This only applies when using the Standard Deviation or Variance Average
methods, and does not apply when using the GapFill or Float methods.




The above settings are maintatined individually for each Cacti user.  The
default values can be changed globally in the Cacti settings, found at
Configuration > Settings > Spikes.  A user can change her or his individual
settings by selecting different choices in the Spikekill menu which appears
next to a graph.


Detailed Operation


Independence between each RRA:DS pair


When Spikekill runs, it analyzes each data source (DS) of each round-robin
archive (RRA) independently.  During analysis, it calculates values for
average and standard deviation for each RRA:DS pair.


To understand this better, consider the following example.


* You have a round-robin database (RRD) file that stores traffic levels on a
router interface.


* You have two DSes, which are traffic_in and traffic_out.


* You also have selected three profiles, which are 1 minute for 7 days, 15
minutes for 5 weeks, and 1 hour for 3 years.


* Lastly, you have three consolidation functions (CF), which are average,
min, and max.




In this example, you have 9 RRAs (3 profiles multiplied by 3 CFs).  Therefore,
Spikekill will calculate 18 averages and 18 standard deviation values (9 RRAs
multiplied by 2 DSes).  Data points for each RRA:DS pair will be compared
against their respective average and standard deviation values.


Search order


Spikekill searches for spikes in the same order as rrddump exports data,
which is sequentially by RRA, from oldest to newest data points within each
RRA.  Data points are overwritten if they exceed the maximum allowed threshold
or if they do not reach the minimum required threshold.


Spikekill will cease overwriting data points for any individual RRA when it has
reached the value of Kills Per RRA, counting all DSes of that RRA together.
It will then begin the process again on the next RRA.


This means that for any given RRA, older spikes will always be overwritten
before newer spikes.  However, newer spikes in one RRA can still be overwritten
before older spikes in a different RRA, since the thresholds are different per
RRA:DS pair.




Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


Settings


Cacti has numerous settings that can be changed from the Web
Interface.  Cacti, from it's inception desired to have as many settings
as possible to be configurable without having to goto the Command Line to
make them.  While, for the most part this is still true, there are
several settings that must be configured in the config.php which we
will cover in a separate sub-section.


The standard Settings sub-tabs include:


* General <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Settings-General.md> - This is where Cacti holds a general
collection of settings that every Cacti administrator should become
familiar with.


* Paths <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Settings-Paths.md> - Mostly, this sub-tab holds all settings
that the Cacti administrator has control of.  It also includes some
settings around RRDfile maintenance.


* Device Defaults <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Settings-Device-Defaults.md> - This is where you can
establish
presets that every new Cacti Device gets by default, through
Automation, the CLI or though the Web Interface.


* Poller <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Settings-Poller.md> - Though most Poller settings have been
moved to the Data Collectors, some are still managed at the system
level including the poller type to use, script server and a number
of additional settings.


* Data <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Settings-Data.md> - This is where you can enable the RRDproxy
and Data Source Statistics collection.  The Data Source Statistics
module allows for the saving of key metrics that are important in
dash-boarding plugins in Cacti.


* Visual <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Settings-Visual.md> - This is where you can set some preset
like Graph and Data Source title lengths, and drop-down sizes
these Visual settings are global in nature.


* Authentication <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Settings-Auth.md> - This is where Cacti
defines how Users are managed.  The available methods depend on the Cacti
version.


They include:


* Local Users <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Settings-Auth-Local.md> - Authentication and Authorization
are handled in Cacti. Passwords are stored as hashes.

* Basic Users <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Settings-Auth-Basic.md> - Authentication outside of Cacti,
for example SAML2, TACACS+, NIS, etc.). Authorization remains in Cacti

* LDAP Users <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Settings-Auth-LDAP.md> - Authentication outside of Cacti,
either LDAP or Active Directory.  Authorization remains in Cacti

* Login Providers <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Login-Providers.md> - LDAP, Active Directory, SAML2, and
OpenID Connect on the unreleased development branch.

* Multiple Domains <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Settings-Domains.md> - Cacti 1.2 directory domains with
their own authorization templates.



* Performance <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Settings-Performance.md> - This is where Cacti's Boost
functionality was moved to.  Cacti's Boost module allows Cacti to
scale to systems with over a million Graphs and Data Sources.


* Spikes <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Settings-Spikes.md> - This is a built-in tool for killing
spikes on graphs in a scheduled fashion.  It also holds system level
spike killing defaults.


* Mail/Reporting/DNS <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Settings-Mail-Reporting-DNS.md> - This is where
Cacti saves settings associated with these three services, for example
Simple Mail Transfer Protocol (SMTP) settings used for general
notification across all of Cacti.




In addition, to the base settings, Cacti Plugins can extend settings
for their own purposes.  When a Plugin has few settings
that it uses, the Cacti Group has recommended a sub-tab called
Misc for those Plugins for their purposes.  Most Plugins
conform to that guideline.




Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


General Settings


General Settings in Cacti consist of the following categories:


* Log Settings - This is where you setup Cacti's logging

* Internationalization (i18n) - Settings around how Cacti handles languages
other than English

* Other Settings - Other not object settings in Cacti

* Site Security - Web Site Security settings such as
Content-Security-Policy,
use of external JavaScript API's for plugins like Google Maps, forcing https,
etc.

* Automation - Miscellaneous settings around Cacti's Automation

* Graph Template Defaults - Defaults for Graph Templates



Each of these settings will be documented below.


Log Settings


Cacti has the ability to log at various levels for various files.  It has the
ability
to log to both the standard cacti.log file, as well as sending some
notifications
to Syslog for forwarding to centralized logging servers.  The image below
show what this settings sub-tab section looks like:


[Image: Log Settings] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/settings-general-log-settings.png>


Those settings include:


* Log Destination - Where to place Cacti log output.
Options include:


* Logfile Only - Only send log data to the Cacti log file

* Logfile and Syslog - Send log data to both the Cacti log file and the
System log facility

* Syslog Only - Send select log data to just the System log facility



This setting is quite self explanatory, when it comes to what type of
messages
are sent to the System log facility, see the Syslog/Eventlog Item Selection
section below.


* General Log Level - This is the level of logging that Cacti will use.
Options include:


* NONE - Don't perform any logging, not recommended.

* LOW - Statistics and Errors

* MEDIUM - Statistics, Errors and Results

* HIGH - Statistics, Errors, Results and Major I/O Events

* DEBUG - Statistics, Errors, Results, I/O and Program Flow

* DEVEL - Developer level messages, mainly SQL Queries



Select the log level that works best for your logging needs.  Be advised that
DEBUG generates a large amount of log data.  DEVEL should only log
SQL Queries.


* Log Input Validation Issues - This is a Developer option to log the use of
request variables that have not been validated through Cacti's validation
functions.


* Data Source Tracing - This is a debug options for logging of program
flow around the creation of Data Sources.


* Selective File Debug - A Multi-select setting that allows you to place
any Cacti file to be placed into debug mode.  This prevents the cacti.log
file from being flooded with messages as is common in a general debug
context.


* Selective Plugin Debug - For Plugins, the same level of debug that is
available for core Cacti files are available for Plugin files as well.


* Device Debug - This is a comma delimited list of Cacti device_id that
debugging of Data Collection services will be enabled for.


* Syslog/Eventlog Item Selection - In order to not saturate the
System logging facility, Cacti will only forward some events as defined
by the various check-boxes.




Internationalization (i18n)


The image below shows system level Internationalization settings for Cacti,
commonly
referred to as i18n.


[Image: Internationalization Settings] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/settings-general-i18n.png>


Those settings include:


* Language Support - Whether or not to allow selecting other languages for
Cacti


* Language - The default language to use at login time, or by default
when the user has not specified a language.


* Auto Language Detection - Whether or not Cacti should read the language
from the browser and use that language by default.


* Preferred Language Processor - Some PHP distributions doe not come with
the php-gettext module.  In those cases, the various Linux distributions
come with alternate GetText processors.  You can select the one that works
best for your environment here.


* Date Display Format - The default Date/Time display format.


* Date Separator - The default Data/Time separator character.




Other Settings


These are additional settings for Cacti that fall into various categories, but
that
done have enough entries to justify a separate section.  The image below shows
that
sub-section.


[Image: Other Settings] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/settings-general-other-settings.png>


Those settings include:


* Has Graphs/Data Sources Checked - If from the Console, you enter
a pages that has the various Has options, if checked here, the default
will be to check that setting.


* RRDtool Version - Selecting the correct RRDtool Version will unlock
features
expected in modern graphing frameworks that leverage SVG (Canvas) rendering.


* Graph Permission Method - The development branch offers Permissive,
Restrictive, Device Based, and Graph Template Based policies. These combine
direct graph access with device and template policies in different ways.
See User Management <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/User-Management.md#graph-permissions> and verify the
affected user's effective permissions before changing the global method.


* Graph/Data Source Creation Method - If you wish to only create Graphs
from Graph Templates choose the Simple method.  The Advanced
method should only be used by people who wish to define individual Graphs
with their own settings.


* Show Form/Setting Help Inline - If this option is checked, you will be
able to read all form/setting help inline.  Otherwise, you will receive a
pop-up to access form help.


* Delete Verification - For simple object removal, things that can be
removed
by clicking and 'X' on the page, whether or not you wish to confirm those
deletes.


* Data Source Preservation Preset - Whether or not you wish to preserve
Data Sources by default, or remove them when deleting Graphs. In
all cases, Cacti will not allow the removal of a Data Source if there
are Graphs using the Data Source.  However, some Data Sources
are managed externally to Cacti, and removing those Data Sources by
default may not be desired.


* Graph Auto Unlock - If when editing a Graph you wish to always unlock
it by default, select this option.  Not that this does have performance
implications that may make the User Interface slower on vary large systems
where devices have tens of thousands of Data Sources.


* Hide Cacti Dashboard - This is an option used by some plugin developers
who replace the default Cacti Console with their own contents, for example
the Intropage plugin.


* Enable Drag-N-Drop - Certain Cacti pages allow Drag-N-Drop.  If you
with to use it, check that option.




Site Security


The image below shows Site Security settings for Cacti.


[Image: Site Security] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/settings-general-site-security.png>


Those settings include:


* Force Connections over HTTPS - Even if the web site allows http, redirect
their browsers to HTTPS.


* Content-Security Allow Unsafe JavaScript eval() calls - Allow plugins to
use
the JavaScript eval() function call, which is historically unsafe.
However,
some older plugins require it.


* Content-Security Alternate Sources - If for some reason your Cacti site
requires content from a CDN or a third party site, you can include a space
delimited list of URLs that will be able to be traversed for Cacti content.




Automation Settings


The image below shows general Automation Settings for Cacti.


[Image: Site Security] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/settings-general-automation.png>


Those settings include:


* Enable Automatic Graph Creation - If a Cacti Device has Non Data
Query
Graph Templates associated with it, create those Graphs automatically
when
adding a new Device.  Additionally, if you have Graph Rules apply
those
at Device creation time and save time.


* Enable Automatic Tree Item Creation - If you have Cacti Tree Rules
apply
then at Device creation and save time.


* Automation Notification To Email - When Network Automation finishes, send
a notification to this Email address.


* Automation Notification From Name - The From Name on the Automation
Email.


* Automation From Email - The From Email such as noreply@cacti.net that
will be used for Automation notifications.




Graph Template Defaults


This is where Cacti obtains defaults for Graph Templates.


[Image: Graph Template Defaults] <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/images/settings-general-graph-template-defaults.png>


* Graph Template Image Format - When creating a new Graph Template
what Image Format should be used.  There is either:


SVG - Scalable Vector Graphics, which retain detail when scaled.
PNG - Portable Network Graphics, a raster image format.


* Graph Template Height - The height in pixels of the Graph Canvas for new
Graph Templates


* Graph Template Width - The width in pixels of the Graph Canvas for new
Graph Templates
