Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


Contributing


Cacti accepts bug reports, code changes, documentation corrections, and
translations. Choose the repository that owns the affected component:


* Cacti <https://github.com/Cacti/cacti> for the application.

* Documentation <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation> for this manual.

* Spine <https://github.com/Cacti/spine> for the C poller.

* The relevant Cacti plugin repository <https://github.com/orgs/Cacti/repositories>
for a plugin.



Report a problem


Search the project's open and closed issues before opening a report. Include
versions, reproduction steps, expected and actual behavior, and relevant logs.
Remove passwords, SNMP communities, tokens, and other sensitive information.
See Known Issues <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Known-Issues.md> for release notes and existing reports.
For installation or usage help, use the Cacti
Forums <https://forums.cacti.net/>.


Report suspected vulnerabilities privately through the
Cacti security policy <https://github.com/Cacti/cacti/security/policy>.


Code contributions


Create a branch for one focused change and submit a pull request to the
repository that owns it. Use develop unless maintainers request another
branch. Check the issue and target branch before starting a maintenance fix;
a change needed on one branch may already be fixed on another.


Read the contributor instructions in that branch. In the Cacti development
repository, composer.json defines PHP requirements and checks, .nvmrc
specifies the Node version, and package-lock.json locks frontend dependencies.
For a development checkout, install dependencies from the repository root:


composer install
npm ci



Use the branch's formatter, static analysis, and relevant tests before
submitting. The development branch provides composer check; consult its
contributor guide <https://github.com/Cacti/cacti/blob/develop/CONTRIBUTING.md>
for individual commands. Maintenance branches may have different tools.
Describe what changed and how you verified it. Add regression coverage when
it protects against a realistic failure, and link the associated issue.


Follow the code formatting <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Standards-Code-Formatting.md>,
PHP <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Standards-PHP-Spec-Constructs.md>, SQL <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Standards-SQL.md>, and
security <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Standards-Security.md> guides. Update relevant documentation and
follow the target branch's changelog instructions. See
Patch Creation <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Standards-Patch-Creation.md> for the submission workflow.


Documentation contributions


Submit manual changes to
the documentation repository <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation>.
Explain the correction and link an existing issue where applicable. Check
instructions against the Cacti branch being documented, distinguish released
features from development features, and verify commands, links, and images.


Follow the Documentation Standards <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Standards-Documentation.md> and link new
pages from README.md. Updating this repository does not immediately update
the public website; publication depends on the project's documentation build
and deployment process.


Translations


Use the translation guide <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Contributing-Translations.md> to contribute through
Cacti's translation service. Changes to translatable application strings must
also update the source message template as described in that guide.




Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


Creating Translations


Translate Cacti and its plugins


Create an account at the Cacti Translation Site <https://translate.cacti.net/>,
select the project and branch, and choose a language. Review existing strings
or add missing translations through the site's Weblate interface.


Weblate manages synchronization of language translations with GitHub. Do not
include unrelated language catalog changes in a code pull request.


Update the source message template


Wrap user-facing strings with Cacti's translation helpers, such as __().
When adding, changing, or removing translatable strings, update
locales/po/cacti.pot in the same pull request.


From the Cacti application repository root, use its extraction script:


./locales/update-pot.sh
msgfmt --check-format -o /dev/null locales/po/cacti.pot



The script requires GNU gettext (xgettext); msgfmt validates the template.
Review the resulting diff and keep it focused on the changed strings. The
script and extraction coverage can differ by branch, so confirm that each new
or changed message appears in the template.


Commit the source template, locales/po/cacti.pot, with your code change.
Leave per-language .po and compiled .mo updates to Weblate.


Catalog locations and local testing


Cacti stores the source template and language .po files under locales/po/.
Compiled runtime catalogs are under locales/LC_MESSAGES/.


For local testing, use a separate Cacti installation, compile the relevant
catalog using gettext, and select the language in your user profile. Preserve
the repository's locale names and directory layout. Do not deploy catalog
files from an unrelated branch to a production installation.


You can edit translations with a gettext-compatible editor such as
Poedit <https://poedit.net/>, then submit them through Weblate. Consult the
Weblate upload documentation <https://docs.weblate.org/en/latest/user/files.html>
for supported formats and upload options.


Preserve placeholders, plural forms, and formatting markers when translating.
Test messages with substituted values as well as messages containing accented
or non-Latin characters.




Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


Documentation Standards


Write instructions that match the Cacti branch being documented. State when a
feature is available only on the development branch or in a particular release.
Verify commands, configuration names, links, and screenshots against that
version. Keep changes focused on the affected topic.


Source and corrections


This manual is maintained in the
Cacti documentation repository <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation>.
Submit corrections there, linking an existing issue when applicable. Use
develop unless maintainers request a maintenance branch. A repository change
reaches the public website through the project's build and deployment process.


Markdown format


The repository's .mdlrc loads .mdl_style.rb. These files define the local
Markdown checks. Preserve the existing page's style where it does not conflict
with those checks.


Titles


Use ATX headings with a space after the hash characters. Use one level-one
heading for the page title, then organize sections with levels two through five.
Use level six for numbered example, figure, or table captions when needed.
Avoid punctuation at the end of headings and keep heading text descriptive.


# Page title

## Main section

### Subsection

Paragraph text.



Separate headings from surrounding content with a blank line. Preserve existing
heading anchors when possible; when renaming a heading, update links to it.
Empty HTML anchors may preserve public fragment links when headings move or
change. Use Markdown for visible links and ordinary page content.


Text and lists


Wrap prose at 79 characters. Leave one blank line between paragraphs and around
lists and code blocks. Do not leave trailing whitespace or repeated blank
lines. Do not use raw HTML for ordinary page content.


Use - for unordered lists, a number followed by a full stop for ordered lists,
and consistent indentation for nested items and continuation text.


- First item.
- Second item with a continuation that remains part of the same list item.

1. First step.
2. Second step.



Use backticks for file paths, commands, configuration keys, and literal values.
Use bold text for interface labels where it improves readability.


Code blocks


Use fenced code blocks with a language label such as console, bash, php,
sql, or ini. Keep example commands reproducible and identify placeholders.
Use continuation lines for long commands where the shell supports them. Code
and table widths are exempt from the prose line-length rule in the current
style configuration.


Do not include real credentials, tokens, private keys, or unredacted logs.
Explain required privileges and commands that alter or delete stored data.


Links and images


Use Markdown links with meaningful labels. Use relative .md paths for manual
pages so the links work in the repository and published manual.


[Data Collectors](Data-Collectors.md)
[Collection settings](Settings-Poller.md#general-settings)
![Data Collectors](images/data-collectors.png)



Check that target files and heading anchors exist. Add new guides to
Manual-Index.md and link entry pages from README.md. Check image filenames
and use alternate text that describes the image. Explain when a screenshot
shows an older interface or a specific theme.


Tables


Use Markdown tables with a header and separator row. Keep column names and
units explicit. Add a caption when the table needs to be referenced elsewhere.


Copyright


End pages with a separator and the Cacti Group copyright notice, following the
target branch's current year:


---

Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group



Validate changes


Install Ruby and the mdl gem, then run checks from the documentation root:


gem install mdl
bin/check_markdown_style.sh Changed-Page.md
bin/check_missing_link.sh



Omit the filename to run the Markdown check across the repository. Existing
files may have unrelated lint failures; distinguish those from failures added
by the change. check_missing_link.sh checks whether Markdown filenames occur
in README.md; it does not validate every link target or heading anchor.
Check those separately.


The repository's GitHub Actions spelling workflow installs the pinned
requirements from .github/codespell-requirements.txt. To run the same spelling
check in a Python virtual environment:


python3 -m venv /tmp/cacti-docs-spelling
/tmp/cacti-docs-spelling/bin/python -m pip install \
  --only-binary :all: --require-hashes \
  -r .github/codespell-requirements.txt
/tmp/cacti-docs-spelling/bin/codespell --config .codespell.cfg



Review git diff --check and the rendered Markdown before submitting. Verify
that commands and menus describe the intended version; passing Markdown and
spelling checks does not establish technical accuracy.




Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


Formatting Guidelines


Overview


Follow the target branch's .php-cs-fixer.php and contributor instructions.
The development branch exposes composer php-cs-fixer for a dry run and
composer php-cs-fixit to apply formatting. Inspect formatter changes and
avoid unrelated formatting in a focused fix.


Cacti uses conventions based on
PSR-12 <https://www.php-fig.org/psr/psr-12/> with project-specific exceptions.
The checked-in formatter configuration takes precedence over these examples.


The main conventions are:


* Initial white space must be tabs not spaces followed by the use of spaces to
align elements after initial tab.


* In a block of variable assignments or array elements (moved to multi-line) it
is acceptable, but not required, to use spaces to align the = or => for
purpose of readability.


* Bracketing/braces should start on the same line of the statement.  For
example:


* if (expression) {,

* } else {,

* and } elseif (expression) {



* Use lower case constants for things like false, true, and null.


* Use print over echo.


* Function declarations should include opening braces on the same line as
the function.  For example:


* function name($params ...) {



* White-space inside of evaluations is discouraged.  For example:


* if (expression) { and not if ( expression ) {.



* Prefer single quotes for literal strings. Use double quotes for interpolation
or escape sequences. Use short array syntax on the development branch;
preserve compatibility with the target branch.




All style guidelines should be followed exactly as shown in these examples,
unless indicated otherwise.


Indenting


Very simply, always use tabs to indent rather than spaces.
Tab stops should be set to 4 spaces.


Below are the vim rules to accomplish this:


set noexpandtab
set shiftwidth=4
set softtabstop=4
set tabstop=4



Control Structures


Use the following style guidelines for control structures.


if elseif else Construct


if ($var == 0) {
    $i++;
} elseif ($var == 1) {
    $i--;
} else {
    $i = 0;
}



switch Construct


switch ($var) {
    case 0:
        $var = 1;

        break;
    case 1:
        $var = 2;

        break;
    default:
        $var = 3;

        break;
}



while Loop


while ($i < 10) {
    $i++;
}



for Loop


for ($i = 0; $i < 10; $i++) {
    $var *= 6;
}



foreach Loop


foreach ($array as $key => $value) {
    print "$key = $value\n";
}



Naming


General Function Naming


All function names should begin with an identifier (see below) and end with
an action.
The identifier should generally be derived from the name of the file which
the function resides.


Returns all users


function user_list() {
    // code
}



Returns a specific user


function user_get($user_id) {
    // code
}



Returns the total number of graphs


function graph_total_get() {
    // code
}



Returns a specific graph title


function graph_title_get() {
    // code
}



Constant Naming


Constants should always be all capital letters. In most cases, they begin
with some identifier. The name should be verbose enough that it describes
exactly what the constant represents. Below is the general structure of the
name.


[identifier]_[type]_[value]


Examples of application constants


define('DATA_INPUT_TYPE_SCRIPT', 1);
define('DATA_INPUT_TYPE_SNMP', 2);



Data input types for data sources


define('DATA_INPUT_TYPE_SNMP_QUERY', 3);
define('DATA_INPUT_TYPE_SCRIPT_QUERY', 4);



Common Action Types


* list - an array of one or more items


* get - an array with one item or a single value (such as an integer or string)


* remove - some data is to be removed from the database


* update - some pre-existing data is to be updated


* insert - some new data to be saved


* print - information will be echoed to the screen/browser


* generate - data will be generated and returned


* format - data will be passed in, formatted, and then returned




Formatting


Argument Spacing


Always include a single space after each argument in a list except for the last
one.


function log_insert($facility, $severity, $message) {
    // code
}



log_insert(LOG_FACILITY_LOCAL0, LOG_LEVEL_WARNING, 'test message');



As a general rule of thumb, it is OK to include additional spaces where
necessary to improve code readability.
The key here is that writing readable code always takes priority.


White space


Always place a single blank line after a control structure unless the following
line is an ending brace }.  There should be no blank lines at the end of the
file, and no spaces at the end of line.


if ($var == 0) {
    $i = 0;
}

if ($i == 0) {
    $i *= 2;
}



if ($var ==0) {
    if ($i == 2) {
        $i++;
    }
}



Storage Constructs


Constants


Always use constants instead of numbers to represent static values. Instead of
doing:


if ($poller_verbosity == 2) {
    // code
}



Do:


if ($poller_verbosity == POLLER_VERBOSITY_LOW) {
    // code
}



Variables


Use a local variable when it clarifies intent, avoids repeated work, or makes
error handling explicit. Do not sacrifice readability to avoid an assignment.


Global Variables


Use explicit arguments and return values for new shared code where practical.
Preserve existing global-state contracts when fixing legacy functions. Do not
introduce additional global state as an incidental part of a focused change.


References


Pass arrays by value unless the function is intended to modify the caller's
array. PHP uses copy-on-write; adding references is not a general performance
optimization. Document intentional mutation and measure performance-sensitive
changes against the actual call path.


Database Calls


Ensure that you indent your Database calls by a single tab per indentation level
and that you break up database calls by the various provisos.  A few well
defined
database queries can be seen in the examples below


$devices = db_fetch_assoc_prepared('SELECT *
  FROM host
  WHERE id = ?',
  [$device_id]);

$vdefs = db_fetch_assoc("SELECT rs.*,
  SUM(CASE WHEN local_graph_id = 0 THEN 1 ELSE 0 END) AS templates,
  SUM(CASE WHEN local_graph_id > 0 THEN 1 ELSE 0 END) AS graphs
  FROM (
    SELECT vd.*, gti.local_graph_id
    FROM vdef AS vd
    LEFT JOIN graph_templates_item AS gti
    ON gti.vdef_id = vd.id
    GROUP BY vd.id, gti.graph_template_id, gti.local_graph_id
  ) AS rs
  $sql_where
  GROUP BY rs.id
  $sql_having
  $sql_order
  $sql_limit");



* Use prepared statements for values, as in the first example.


* Build dynamic identifiers and SQL fragments from explicit allowlists.
Do not concatenate untrusted values into SQL.


* Always use the term AS to define an alias.


* Ensure that your JOINs are either INNER, LEFT, or RIGHT.


* Ensure that the ON clause uses table aliases, and that each
AND or OR expression is logically organized for easy reading
and interpretation.


* In your SQL WHERE and SELECT, where functions are involved


* Ensure that there are spaces between parameter

* Ensure operators and variables are separated by spaces





Comments


Explain intent, constraints, and non-obvious behavior. Keep comments consistent
with the code and avoid comments that merely restate an assignment or test.


Whole Line Comments


All single and multi-line comments should follow the C-style syntax.


/* this determines if $i is equal to 0 */
if ($i == 0) {
    // code
}



/* this is an extra long comment that deserves to be
 * split up into multiple lines
 */
if ($i == 0) {
    // code
}



End of Line Comments


Shorter comments that follow a line of code should use the familiar // style.


$var = 0; // assign 0 to $var



File Header Comments


All code related files must contain the following notice at the beginning of
the file.


/*
 +-------------------------------------------------------------------------+
 | Copyright (C) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group                                 |
 |                                                                         |
 | This program is free software; you can redistribute it and/or           |
 | modify it under the terms of the GNU General Public License             |
 | as published by the Free Software Foundation; either version 2          |
 | of the License, or (at your option) any later version.                  |
 |                                                                         |
 | This program is distributed in the hope that it will be useful,         |
 | but WITHOUT ANY WARRANTY; without even the implied warranty of          |
 | MERCHANTABILITY or FITNESS FOR A PARTICULAR PURPOSE.  See the           |
 | GNU General Public License for more details.                            |
 +-------------------------------------------------------------------------+
 | Cacti: The Complete RRDtool-based Graphing Solution                     |
 +-------------------------------------------------------------------------+
 | This code is designed, written, and maintained by the Cacti Group. See  |
 | about.php and/or the AUTHORS file for specific developer information.   |
 +-------------------------------------------------------------------------+
 | https://www.cacti.net/                                                   |
 +-------------------------------------------------------------------------+
*/



Function Comments


All functions must be preceded by a comment describing the purpose, argument
list, and return type of the function. It should be formatted per
PHP DocBlock specifications as follow:


/**
 * Makes a connection to the database server
 *
 * Given a set of login credentials, an attempt is made to establish a persistent database connection.
 * If the attempt fails, script execution will halt with an error.
 *
 * @param string $host the hostname of the database server, 'localhost' if the database server is running
 *   on this machine
 * @param string $user the username to connect to the database server as
 * @param string $pass the password to connect to the database server with
 * @param string $db_name the name of the database to connect to
 * @param string $db_type the type of database server to connect to, only 'mysql' is currently supported
 * @param int $retries the number a time the server should attempt to connect before failing
 *
 * @return bool true if successful, false otherwise
 */



Place a space between the @param and @return.




Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


PHP Specific Constructs


Use the PHP range declared in the target branch's composer.json, together
with its CI configuration. The development branch requires PHP 8.3 or later
within the declared Composer range; maintenance branches can differ.
Do not introduce syntax or APIs unsupported by the branch being changed.


Traversing arrays


Use foreach to traverse arrays. each() was removed in PHP 8.0.


foreach ($devices as $device) {
	// Process the device.
}



Define the expected type at the function boundary. If an existing API can
return false or null, handle that result before iterating or calling
count(). Use is_countable() when a value can be an array or Countable.


Return values


Preserve the function's documented return contract. Compare strictly when
false signals failure and a successful value could be 0 or an empty array.
Do not change a public API's error handling as part of an unrelated fix.


$result = file_get_contents($path);

if ($result === false) {
	// Handle the read failure.
}



String operations and regular expressions


Use string functions for literal operations and preg_* functions when the
operation requires a regular expression. The old ereg* and split() APIs
are unavailable on supported development runtimes.


Prefer single quotes for literal strings. Use double quotes when interpolation
or escape sequences make the code clearer. Do not claim a performance benefit
without measuring the relevant code path.


Including PHP files


Web entry points that require an authenticated user load include/auth.php.
User-facing CLI entry points load include/cli_check.php. These bootstrap
files establish the application environment; a CLI entry point must not load
the web authentication bootstrap.


After bootstrap, resolve library paths relative to $config['base_path']:


require_once($config['base_path'] . '/lib/api_device.php');



Use require_once for required libraries that might already be loaded. Follow
the existing entry point's --help and --version conventions for CLI tools.
Keep request validation and authorization at the appropriate boundaries; see
Security <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Standards-Security.md>.




Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


File System Layout


Use the target branch's existing structure and naming conventions when adding
files. Some names, including host for device-related tables and pages, are
historical compatibility contracts. Do not rename them as incidental cleanup.


Application entry points


PHP pages in the repository root handle web requests, for example host.php,
graphs.php, and data_sources.php. Authenticated pages load
include/auth.php. Administrative command-line tools live under cli/ and
load include/cli_check.php.


The root also contains polling entry points such as poller.php, cmd.php,
script_server.php, and cactid.php. These have different responsibilities;
keep scheduling separate from individual data collection scripts.


Shared code and configuration


* include/ contains bootstrap, authentication, configuration, global arrays,
settings, and form definitions. Configure an installation in
include/config.php, using the supplied distribution file as a reference.

* lib/ contains shared functions and application classes. Existing api_*
files provide management operations; html_* files provide rendering and
form helpers. Keep reusable operations independent of request superglobals.

* lib/Auth/ and lib/Console/ contain namespaced classes mapped by Composer
on the development branch. Check composer.json before adding a namespace.

* include/vendor/ is the Composer installation directory. Declare PHP
dependencies in composer.json and commit the lockfile; do not commit
installed package trees or edit generated autoload files.



Load dependencies relative to the application base path after bootstrap:


require_once($config['base_path'] . '/lib/api_device.php');



Frontend assets


* include/themes/ contains themes.

* include/js/ contains JavaScript assets used by the application.

* images/ contains application images.

* build/ contains frontend build instructions and tooling on the development
branch. Follow its README and the locked npm dependencies when rebuilding
assets.



Data collection and extensions


* resource/ contains XML data-query definitions and other resources.

* scripts/ contains data collection scripts.

* plugins/ contains one directory per installed plugin. The directory name
must match the plugin's expected identifier, and a plugin supplies an INFO
file describing it.

* locales/po/ contains cacti.pot and language source catalogs;
locales/LC_MESSAGES/ contains compiled catalogs. Follow the
translation workflow <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Contributing-Translations.md>.



Runtime files


rra/, log/, and cache/ are conventional locations for RRD files, logs,
and cached output. Actual paths depend on installation settings. Give the
poller and web server only the access they need; do not make application code
writable to solve a runtime-directory permission problem.


Tests and documentation


The development branch keeps PHP tests under tests/, with unit tests grouped
by domain under tests/Unit/. Follow the target branch's contributor
instructions for test placement, bootstrap, and commands.


Application documentation is under docs/; this manual is maintained in the
separate documentation repository <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation>.
Link new manual pages from its README.md and use existing image paths where
appropriate.




Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


Patch Creation


Submit changes as a GitHub pull request to the repository that owns the code
or documentation. See Contributing <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Contributing.md> to choose a repository.


Choose the target branch


Use develop unless maintainers request another branch. Confirm the target
before starting a maintenance fix. Check that the defect exists on that
branch and that the change preserves its runtime and dependency compatibility.


Keep independent changes in separate pull requests. If a change depends on an
unmerged pull request, target its branch and describe that dependency. Keep
stack branches in the same repository and merge from the bottom up.


Create a working branch


Fork the relevant repository on GitHub, then clone your fork. This example is
for the Cacti application; substitute documentation or spine when needed.
Replace YOUR-USERNAME with your GitHub username.


git clone https://github.com/YOUR-USERNAME/cacti.git
cd cacti
git remote add upstream https://github.com/Cacti/cacti.git
git fetch upstream
git switch -c fix/device-description upstream/develop



For a maintenance contribution, create the branch from the agreed upstream
maintenance branch instead. Configure your Git author identity locally if it
is not already configured; do not copy another contributor's identity.


Make and validate the change


Keep the patch focused on one problem. Update affected documentation and add
regression coverage for realistic failures. Review the target branch's
contributor instructions, install its locked dependencies, and run the relevant
formatter, analysis, and tests.


For the Cacti development branch, the
contributor guide <https://github.com/Cacti/cacti/blob/develop/CONTRIBUTING.md>
and composer.json describe the available checks. For this manual, follow
Documentation Standards <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Standards-Documentation.md>. Maintenance branches may
have different checks; do not introduce new tooling solely for a backport.


Follow the target branch's changelog policy. Where changelog.d/ fragments are
used, follow its README. If maintainers require a direct root CHANGELOG edit,
use the current unreleased section and the real associated issue or advisory
identifier. Keep security entries before issues and issues before features,
placing a numbered issue among its numeric neighbors. Do not put a new fix in
an already released version.


Inspect the patch before committing:


git diff --check
git diff
git status --short



Stage the intended files explicitly and write a commit message that explains
the change. Avoid committing generated dependencies, credentials, or unrelated
formatting changes.


Submit the pull request


Push your working branch to your fork:


git push -u origin fix/device-description



Open a pull request in the upstream repository. Confirm both the base branch
and the file diff before submitting. Explain the problem, resulting behavior,
and validation, and link the associated issue. Identify version-specific
behavior or limitations that matter to reviewers.


A pull request with no reported checks is unverified. Fork workflows may wait
for a maintainer's approval; distinguish pending or missing checks from passing
checks. Address review feedback on the same branch.


Update the branch


Fetch upstream changes and rebase a branch you own onto its intended base when
needed. Resolve conflicts by preserving both changes' behavior, then rerun the
checks relevant to the resolution. Recheck changelog placement and duplicate
entries after resolving a changelog conflict.


If you must replace a previously pushed branch after a rebase, use
git push --force-with-lease only for a branch you own and coordinate with
anyone else using it. Do not rewrite shared target branches.




Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


SQL Standards


Cacti uses MySQL or MariaDB through the helpers in lib/database.php. Check
the target branch's supported database versions before adding SQL syntax.


Quotes


When writing queries do not use quotes around numeric values. While MySQL is not
strict about types, most DBMS's assume that any value contained within quotes is
a string.


Use single-quoted PHP strings for literal SQL where possible. Bind changing
values with placeholders rather than interpolating them into a query.


Database compatibility


Use SQL supported by both MySQL and MariaDB at the versions required by the
target branch. Cacti already uses database-specific functions, including
CONCAT() and NOW(); this manual does not imply support for other database
engines. Test changes involving collation, grouping, and date handling on the
supported engines where their behavior differs.


SQL Injection


Cacti provides support for prepared statements. You should use these prepared
statement functions for values to avoid the risk of SQL injection
attacks from the Cacti website. Examples include:


// Example prepared statements
$hostname = db_fetch_cell_prepared('SELECT hostname FROM host WHERE id = ?', [$id]);
$host     = db_fetch_row_prepared('SELECT * FROM host WHERE id = ?', [$id]);
$graphs   = db_fetch_assoc_prepared('SELECT * FROM graph_local WHERE host_id = ?', [$id]);

db_execute_prepared('UPDATE host SET description = ? WHERE id = ?', ['Local Machines', $id]);




Placeholders bind values, not table or column names. Map dynamic identifiers,
sort directions, and other SQL fragments from explicit allowlists. Do not
concatenate user-supplied SQL, even when ordinary values are parameterized.


Format of SQL statements


* For short statements, SQL calls may be made on a single row.


* If the SQL statement starts to grow and be unreadable without scrolling, it
should be wrapped onto a continuation line for ease of use and readability.
The following keywords should also be placed at the start of a new line:


* joins statements (excluding JOIN itself which should not be used) (eg,
INNER
JOIN, LEFT JOIN, RIGHT JOIN, OUTER JOIN, CROSS JOIN)


Note: *Any implicit join or JOIN without one of the modifiers should
be
converted to an explicit join with a modifier. When converting the
implicit
join statement, remember to remove the WHERE joining clause.


* AND / OR


* FROM


* WHERE


* ORDER


* GROUP BY


* HAVING


* LIMIT


* prepared statement arrays




* Indentation should consist of one extra tab when any of the following occur:


* continuation lines


* Subqueries




* There should always be 'AS' when defining an alias for any table, field or
query to clearly define the definition of said alias.


* There should always be a space following a comma and before/after an operator.




Example of single line SQL


Before formatting


$templates = db_fetch_assoc('SELECT DISTINCT gt.id, gt.name FROM graph_templates AS gt INNER JOIN graph_templates_graph AS gtg ON gt.id = gtg.graph_template_id INNER JOIN graph_templates_item AS gti ON gtg.graph_template_id=gti.graph_template_id INNER JOIN data_template_rrd AS dtr ON gti.task_item_id=dtr.id INNER JOIN data_template_data AS dtd ON dtd.data_template_id=dtr.data_template_id AND dtd.local_data_id = 0 WHERE gtg.local_graph_id=0 AND dtr.local_data_id = 0 AND dtd.local_data_id = 0 AND dtd.data_input_id in (2,11,12) ORDER BY gt.name;');



Corrected formatting with prepared usage


$templates = db_fetch_assoc_prepared('SELECT DISTINCT gt.id, gt.name
    FROM graph_templates AS gt
    INNER JOIN graph_templates_graph AS gtg
    ON gt.id = gtg.graph_template_id
    INNER JOIN graph_templates_item AS gti
    ON gtg.graph_template_id=gti.graph_template_id
    INNER JOIN data_template_rrd AS dtr
    ON gti.task_item_id=dtr.id
    INNER JOIN data_template_data AS dtd
    ON dtd.data_template_id=dtr.data_template_id
    AND dtd.local_data_id = 0
    WHERE gtg.local_graph_id=0
    AND dtr.local_data_id = 0
    AND dtd.local_data_id = 0
    AND dtd.data_input_id in (?,?,?)
    ORDER BY gt.name',
    [2, 11, 12]);



Example of implicit join


Before formatting


$user_realms = db_fetch_assoc('SELECT ua.id, uar.realm_id
    FROM user_auth ua, user_auth_realms uar
    WHERE ua.id = uar.user_id');



Corrected formatting


$user_realms = db_fetch_assoc('SELECT ua.id, uar.realm_id
    FROM user_auth ua
    INNER JOIN user_auth_realms uar
    ON ua.id = uar.user_id');





Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


Security


Report vulnerabilities privately


Follow the Cacti security policy <https://github.com/Cacti/cacti/security/policy>
for suspected vulnerabilities. Keep exploit details and credentials out of
public issues and pull requests until disclosure is coordinated.


Authentication and authorization


Authenticated web entry points load include/auth.php. Check the user's realm
and object permissions before returning protected data or changing state.
Hiding a button or filtering a browser view does not enforce authorization.
Shared management functions must not assume that a caller owns an object just
because its ID is valid.


Protect cookie-authenticated changes with Cacti's CSRF mechanism. Use POST for
mutations and include the expected __csrf_magic token in forms and AJAX
requests. Do not implement state changes through GET requests.


Request validation


Treat request data, device responses, plugin output, and database content as
untrusted. Validate each value for its intended use. Cacti provides request
helpers in lib/html_utility.php:


* get_filter_request_var() (also gfrv()) validates an integer by default
and supports PHP filter constants and options for other types.

* get_request_var() (grv()) retrieves request data; retrieval alone does
not establish that the value is valid for a particular use.

* set_request_var() (srv()) stores a request value; it is not a validator.



A value already validated in the request cache need not be validated again in
every getter. Preserve the validation contract and check lengths, ranges,
allowed choices, and permissions where required. The Log Input Validation
Issues setting helps identify missing validation during development.


SQL statements


Use Cacti's prepared database helpers for values:


$hostname = db_fetch_cell_prepared('SELECT hostname
	FROM host
	WHERE id = ?',
	[$device_id]);



Placeholders bind values, not identifiers or SQL fragments. Build dynamic
column names, sort directions, and table names from an explicit allowlist.
See SQL Standards <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Standards-SQL.md>.


OS commands and paths


Prefer an existing API or process helper over constructing shell commands.
When a shell command is required, validate values and escape each argument
using the target branch's Cacti helpers. On the development branch, use
cacti_escapeshellarg_cmd() for arguments reaching Windows cmd.exe, and
cacti_escapeshellarg() for the supported non-cmd path.
A whole-command escape is not a substitute for argument escaping.


Use configured binary paths and restrict user-selected paths to the intended
location. Check ownership and permissions before reading, replacing, or
deleting files. Do not solve permission errors by making application code or
credential files writable by everyone.


Output escaping


Escape values for the context where they are rendered. HTML text, HTML
attributes, JavaScript, URLs, and shell arguments require different handling.
On the development branch, use html_escape() for HTML text and
html_escape_attr() for attribute values. Check helper availability before
backporting to another branch.


print '<td>' . html_escape($description) . '</td>';



When building browser content, use textContent for plain text and DOM methods
for elements. Do not concatenate untrusted values into HTML, inline event
handlers, or executable JavaScript. Validate dynamic URLs for the intended
protocol and destination before escaping them for an attribute.


For intentionally supported HTML, use the application's reviewed sanitization
path. Sanitize after Markdown or other markup conversion and do not append
untrusted markup afterward. A content security policy supplements these
controls;
it does not replace validation, authorization, or output escaping.


Browser and server checks


Browser validation gives prompt feedback, but enforce validation and
permissions on the server. Test realistic denied-access cases as well as valid
requests when changing a security boundary.


See also


Hardening a Cacti Installation <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Hardening-Cacti-Installation.md> covers
installation permissions and operating-system controls.




Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


Architecture Decision Records


Architecture Decision Records (ADRs) capture decisions that guide Cacti's
long-term architecture. They are concise, reviewable documents rather than
implementation plans.


Status values


* Proposed: under review and not yet binding.

* Accepted: approved for new work.

* Superseded: replaced by a later ADR.



Decisions


* ADR-0001: Establish bounded contexts and framework-independent domain code <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Architecture/0001-bounded-contexts.md>

* ADR-0002: Model installation as durable, resumable install runs <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Architecture/0002-durable-install-runs.md>

* ADR-0003: Provide a versioned plugin extension boundary <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Architecture/0003-plugin-extension-boundary.md>

* ADR-0004: Persist workflow state separately from diagnostic artifacts <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Architecture/0004-workflow-storage-and-observability.md>



Contributing


New ADRs should describe the context, decision, consequences, and links to
the implementation work. An ADR does not replace user, operator, or API
documentation.


ADR-0001: Establish bounded contexts and framework-independent domain code


Status: Proposed


Context


Cacti has grown through a long-lived procedural and class-based codebase.
Several workflows combine HTTP handling, CLI parsing, rendering, persistence,
shell execution, and business rules in the same implementation. This makes
incremental change difficult and exposes internal behavior to plugins.


Decision


New architectural work will identify bounded contexts and keep their domain
logic framework-independent. Initial contexts include Installation, Plugin
Management, Configuration, Authorization, Data Collection, and Template and
Package Management.


Domain code will use PHP 8.1 value objects, backed enums, aggregates, and
domain exceptions where they make rules explicit. Constructors must not access
the database, filesystem, shell, globals, or request state. Infrastructure,
framework components, and legacy helpers will be accessed through application
services and narrowly defined ports.


Consequences


* New code can be tested without web, CLI, or database bootstrap.

* Symfony components may implement infrastructure concerns, but Cacti will not
require a full Symfony application migration.

* Existing code is migrated through adapters; this decision does not require a
flag-day rewrite.

* Public plugin APIs must depend on stable application contracts, not domain
internals or legacy globals.


ADR-0002: Model installation as durable, resumable install runs


Status: Proposed


Context


The installer currently keeps workflow state in mutable global settings and
mixes wizard rendering with side effects. Background timestamp coordination can
hide failures or permit ambiguous ownership of an install attempt.


Decision


Installation and upgrade work will be represented by a durable InstallRun
with a unique identifier, typed options, plan version, explicit state,
operation checkpoints, lease data, progress, and structured failure details.


The lifecycle is validating, ready, running, succeeded, failed, or
cancelled. State transitions are validated and recorded atomically. A worker
holds a renewable lease for a run and executes one idempotent operation at a
time. Web and CLI interfaces submit commands and render run views; they do not
perform installation work directly.


Consequences


* Refreshing a wizard page and querying status are side-effect free.

* Failed runs remain failed until an explicit retry or new run is requested.

* Operations can resume from durable checkpoints after interruption.

* Legacy install_* settings require a temporary compatibility adapter and a
documented removal plan.

External Links


This section will describe External Links in Cacti.


External Links lets administrators add navigation entries for approved URLs.
Choose a destination that permits embedding when using an iframe.


An embedded destination can refuse display through its own frame policy. HTTPS
Cacti pages also cannot freely embed HTTP content. Check the destination and
Cacti content security policy; do not weaken site-wide controls just to embed
an unrelated website.


This feature can be very useful to create a single UI for helpdesk/NOC.

Installing on Rocky Linux 9 and 10 with nginx


Use only the supported operating systems <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Requirements.md#supported-operating-systems> and maintained
dependencies compatible with this Cacti branch.


This source-install example covers the unreleased Cacti 1.3 development branch
on RHEL, Rocky Linux, or AlmaLinux. Read Requirements <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Requirements.md>
first. Run administrative commands as root and adapt paths and accounts to
your deployment. For an existing installation, use
Upgrading Cacti <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Upgrading-Cacti.md>. For package-managed Cacti, follow the
package maintainer's procedure instead of overlaying source files.


Choose the Rocky Linux release and packages


Use the current security-updated minor release of Rocky Linux 9 or 10. This
is a fresh-install procedure; do not reset or switch module streams on an
existing database or PHP deployment without a separate upgrade plan.





	Rocky Linux branch
	PHP and database selection






	9
	Enable the AppStream php:8.3 and mariadb:10.11 streams before installing packages




	10
	Install the AppStream PHP and MariaDB packages directly; this branch does not use module streams





Rocky Linux 9


Inspect the available streams, then select the versions for this example:


dnf module list php mariadb
dnf module enable -y php:8.3 mariadb:10.11



Rocky Linux 10


Check the packages offered by AppStream; do not run dnf module commands:


dnf info php-fpm mariadb-server



Rocky's PHP/FPM guide <https://docs.rockylinux.org/guides/web/php/> explains the difference in packaging, and
its release notes <https://docs.rockylinux.org/releases/release_notes/10_0/> list the major-version package changes.
Other Enterprise Linux distributions can differ in subscriptions, repositories,
and package availability; verify those before adapting this Rocky example.


Install the web and database packages


This example uses MariaDB. MySQL installations should use the package and
service names for their selected MySQL repository. Review the database version
against Cacti's requirements before installation.


dnf install -y nginx php-fpm php-cli php-mysqlnd php-gd php-gmp \
  php-intl php-ldap php-mbstring php-xml php-process php-snmp php-pdo \
  mariadb-server rrdtool net-snmp-utils cronie
php -v
php -m



Additional packages can be required by the chosen source revision. Check
Requirements <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Requirements.md>, including Composer dependencies where
applicable. Do not mix extensions from different PHP versions or repositories.


Configure date.timezone in the PHP configuration used by both CLI and FPM.
Check php --ini for CLI and the installer for web PHP. Review the FPM pool
at /etc/php-fpm.d/www.conf. For this example, retain the apache worker
account and allow nginx to connect through the socket ACL:


user = apache
group = apache
listen = /run/php-fpm/www.sock
listen.acl_users = apache,nginx



Modify the existing entries rather than appending a second pool definition.
The socket ACL grants nginx connection access; Cacti's runtime files must be
writable by the FPM worker and poller accounts. Keep code owned by the
administrator. Validate the pool with php-fpm -t before restarting FPM.


Start the database:


systemctl enable --now mariadb



Follow the installed database vendor's initialization and account-security
procedure. Do not set a root password on the shell command line. Configure only
variables accepted by that database version; use Cacti's installer
recommendations rather than a fixed tuning block. Keep InnoDB doublewrite
protection enabled.


Configure nginx


Configure the FPM pool's listen path and socket ACLs so nginx can connect.
The example uses /run/php-fpm/www.sock; verify that it matches your pool.
Keep the FPM worker account consistent with the permissions you grant later.
Save this example in /etc/nginx/conf.d/cacti.conf, replacing the hostname and
certificate paths:


server {
    listen 80;
    server_name cacti.example.com;
    return 301 https://cacti.example.com$request_uri;
}

server {
    listen 443 ssl;
    server_name cacti.example.com;
    root /usr/share/nginx/html;
    index index.php;
    ssl_certificate /etc/pki/tls/certs/cacti.crt;
    ssl_certificate_key /etc/pki/tls/private/cacti.key;
    ssl_protocols TLSv1.2 TLSv1.3;

    location = / { return 302 /cacti/; }
    location = /cacti { return 301 /cacti/; }

    # nginx does not read Apache .htaccess files.
    location ~ (^|/)\. { deny all; }
    location ~ ^/cacti/(cli|contrib|log|mibs|rra|scripts|service)/ {
        deny all;
    }
    location ~ ^/cacti/cache/(boost|mibcache|realtime|spikekill)/ {
        deny all;
    }
    location ~ ^/cacti/include/(config(_local)?\.php|vendor/.*\.php) {
        deny all;
    }

    location ~ ^/cacti/(.+\.php)$ {
        try_files $uri =404;
        include /etc/nginx/fastcgi_params;
        fastcgi_param SCRIPT_FILENAME $document_root$fastcgi_script_name;
        fastcgi_pass unix:/run/php-fpm/www.sock;
    }
    location /cacti/ { try_files $uri $uri/ =404; }
}



Keep private-path restrictions before the PHP handler and validate them over
HTTP. Static assets under include/vendor can be required by the interface; do
not deny all vendor assets indiscriminately. Keep backups outside every web
root. Restrict setup to administrators until installation is complete.


nginx -t
systemctl enable --now php-fpm nginx



Install and configure Cacti


Install Composer using its official installation procedure <https://getcomposer.org/download/> and
verify the installer as documented there. Run composer install --no-dev
and composer check-platform-reqs --no-dev as the deployment owner in the
staged source tree before deploying it to the web root. Do not substitute
composer update or run dependency scripts as the runtime service account.


Use General Installing Instructions <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/General-Installing-Instructions.md#install-and-configure-cacti> to obtain the intended source,
create a new database and dedicated application account, load timezone data,
import cacti.sql, and configure include/config.php. The examples there use
/var/www/html/cacti; substitute /usr/share/nginx/html/cacti here.


Complete the dependency and filesystem-permission checks before opening the
installer. The database account and operating-system service accounts are
separate identities. Grant runtime write access to the actual FPM and poller
accounts, not automatically to the nginx master process or the database user.
Keep executable code and credentials protected during normal operation.


Open https://cacti.example.com/cacti/ and complete the installer. Configure
one scheduler after installation: either a Cacti-specific cron entry or the
supplied cactid systemd service, using the procedure in the general guide. Do
not run both for the same collector. The five-minute cron example is:


*/5 * * * * apache /usr/bin/php /PATH/TO/cacti/poller.php >/dev/null 2>&1



Replace /PATH/TO/cacti and apache with the installed path and chosen poller
account. Enable crond if using cron. Verify a manual poll under that account,
then check scheduled runs, logs, collector status, and fresh graph samples.


SELinux and containers


Keep SELinux enforcing. For a custom installation path, use persistent file
contexts and allow writes only on Cacti's runtime paths. Review audit denials
and authorize the specific network and executable access needed by FPM,
RRDtool, SNMP tools, and the collector. Do not use setenforce 0 as the
installation procedure. See the RHEL SELinux guide <https://docs.redhat.com/en/documentation/red_hat_enterprise_linux/9/html/using_selinux/index>.


For LXC or Proxmox, retain an unprivileged container and its confinement where
possible. Diagnose the specific ICMP/socket permission or mapped-file access
failure. Adjust only the required capability or policy; do not make the entire
container privileged or set an unconfined AppArmor profile merely to run Cacti.


Optional Spine and proxies


Follow Spine <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Spine.md> for the optional compiled collector. Select a
compatible source or package and the development headers for your OS.
Repository names differ: do not apply Rocky/AlmaLinux crb commands to RHEL
without checking its repository configuration. Configure the Spine database
connection with the same application credentials and restrict access to its
configuration file.


When using a reverse proxy, see the proxy configuration in the general
guide <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/General-Installing-Instructions.md>. A header allowlist alone does not authenticate a proxy; prevent
clients from bypassing the trusted proxy and require it to replace forwarded
client-IP headers.




Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


Installing Cacti on Ubuntu Server with nginx


This fresh-install guide covers Cacti development (1.3) with nginx, PHP-FPM,
and MariaDB. Use the requirements <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Requirements.md> for this Cacti branch.
For an existing installation, follow Upgrading Cacti <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Upgrading-Cacti.md>.
Do not overlay source files onto a package-managed Cacti installation.


Choose a supported Ubuntu release


As of 26 October 2026, the latest LTS is Ubuntu Server 26.04 LTS and the
latest interim release is Ubuntu Server 26.10. Use fully updated packages.
The interim release has a shorter maintenance window; plan its next upgrade
before security support expires. See the LTS release notes <https://documentation.ubuntu.com/release-notes/26.04/>,
interim release notes <https://documentation.ubuntu.com/release-notes/26.10/>, and Ubuntu lifecycle <https://ubuntu.com/about/release-cycle>.





	Ubuntu release
	PHP selection
	PHP-FPM unit and socket






	26.04 LTS
	Distribution PHP 8.5 packages
	php8.5-fpm.service, /run/php/php8.5-fpm.sock




	26.10
	Distribution PHP 8.5 packages
	php8.5-fpm.service, /run/php/php8.5-fpm.sock





The Ubuntu package index <https://packages.ubuntu.com/resolute/php/php-fpm> documents the LTS FPM package; the
interim package index <https://packages.ubuntu.com/stonking/php/php-fpm> lists the corresponding package
for 26.10. Check the package candidates before installation. Vendor OS support
and package availability do not establish that every Cacti plugin has been
tested with that PHP version. Validate the selected Cacti and plugin set on
an isolated installation before production use.


Install nginx, PHP-FPM, and MariaDB


Run administrative commands as root. Enable Ubuntu's Universe repository if
it is not already enabled, then install the packages:


apt-get update
apt-get install -y software-properties-common
add-apt-repository -y universe
apt-get update
apt-cache policy php-fpm php-cli mariadb-server nginx
apt-get install -y nginx mariadb-server rrdtool snmp cron git \
  php-fpm php-cli php-mysql php-snmp php-xml php-mbstring php-gd php-gmp \
  php-zip php-ldap php-intl php-curl php-sqlite3 composer
php -v
php --ini
php -m



Use packages from a consistent Ubuntu release and repository set. Do not
install libapache2-mod-php for this nginx/FPM setup. If Apache is already
bound to the chosen HTTP/HTTPS ports, resolve that conflict before starting
nginx rather than running two servers on the same address and port.


Set date.timezone for the CLI and FPM configurations, normally under
/etc/php/8.5/cli/php.ini and /etc/php/8.5/fpm/php.ini. Verify the FPM pool
at /etc/php/8.5/fpm/pool.d/www.conf uses the intended account and socket:


user = www-data
group = www-data
listen = /run/php/php8.5-fpm.sock
listen.owner = www-data
listen.group = www-data
listen.mode = 0660



Edit the existing entries. The nginx worker must be able to connect to that
socket. Check the actual pool if you changed the account, PHP version, or
socket path. Validate FPM configuration and enable the services:


php-fpm8.5 -t
systemctl enable --now mariadb php8.5-fpm nginx cron



Deploy Cacti and create the database


Run dependency installation as the deployment owner in the source directory,
before installing it under the administrator-owned web root:


git clone --branch develop --depth 1 https://github.com/Cacti/cacti.git cacti
cd cacti
composer install --no-dev
composer check-platform-reqs --no-dev



Record git rev-parse HEAD and deploy that complete tree, including its locked
dependencies, to /var/www/html/cacti. Use administrator privileges only for
placement, ownership, and system configuration.


Follow General Installing Instructions <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/General-Installing-Instructions.md#install-and-configure-cacti> to create the new database
and dedicated application account, load timezone data, import the matching
cacti.sql, and copy/configure include/config.php. These are fresh-install
steps; do not import the default schema over an existing Cacti database.


Use the www-data FPM/poller account for this example's runtime permissions.
Keep code owned by the administrator, configuration readable only by the
required identities, and write access limited to runtime paths. Grant extra
installation/import access only where the installer requests it and remove
that access afterward. Database credentials and OS service accounts are
separate identities.


Configure nginx and HTTPS


Provision a certificate for your hostname using your certificate-management
procedure. Replace cacti.example.com and the certificate/key paths below.
Save this virtual host as /etc/nginx/sites-available/cacti:


server {
    listen 80;
    server_name cacti.example.com;
    return 301 https://cacti.example.com$request_uri;
}

server {
    listen 443 ssl;
    server_name cacti.example.com;
    root /var/www/html;
    index index.php;
    ssl_certificate /etc/ssl/certs/cacti.crt;
    ssl_certificate_key /etc/ssl/private/cacti.key;
    ssl_protocols TLSv1.2 TLSv1.3;

    location = / { return 302 /cacti/; }
    location = /cacti { return 301 /cacti/; }

    # nginx does not process Cacti's Apache .htaccess restrictions.
    location ~ (^|/)\. { deny all; }
    location ~ ^/cacti/(cli|contrib|log|mibs|rra|scripts|service)/ {
        deny all;
    }
    location ~ ^/cacti/cache/(boost|mibcache|realtime|spikekill)/ {
        deny all;
    }
    location ~ ^/cacti/include/(config(_local)?\.php|vendor/.*\.php) {
        deny all;
    }

    location ~ ^/cacti/(.+\.php)$ {
        try_files $uri =404;
        include /etc/nginx/fastcgi_params;
        fastcgi_param SCRIPT_FILENAME $document_root$fastcgi_script_name;
        fastcgi_pass unix:/run/php/php8.5-fpm.sock;
    }
    location /cacti/ { try_files $uri $uri/ =404; }
}



Enable this site once, without overwriting an existing link or configuration:


ln -s /etc/nginx/sites-available/cacti /etc/nginx/sites-enabled/cacti
nginx -t
systemctl reload nginx



Review the packaged default site and other virtual hosts for conflicts. Keep
private-path rules before the PHP handler, leave required static vendor
assets accessible, and store backups outside every web root. Confirm over
HTTP that private files are denied and PHP requests execute rather than
expose source. Restrict installation access to administrators until complete.


Complete installation and schedule polling


Open https://cacti.example.com/cacti/, complete the installer, and check the
CLI and web PHP environments separately. Verify executable paths, timezone
data, filesystem permissions, and optional Spine before enabling collection.


Configure either cron or cactid, using the Ubuntu scheduler guide <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Installing-Under-Ubuntu-Debian.md#schedule-polling>.
Do not run both for one collector. For the default five-minute scheduler,
create /etc/cron.d/cacti with mode 644 and this system-cron entry:


*/5 * * * * www-data /usr/bin/php /var/www/html/cacti/poller.php >/dev/null 2>&1



Run a manual polling cycle as the poller account before scheduling, then
verify scheduled execution, collector status, logs, and recent RRD samples.
Keep AppArmor enforcing and adjust only the specific policy and filesystem
access required by the deployed services. For reverse proxies, use the
trusted-proxy guidance <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/General-Installing-Instructions.md> rather than trusting arbitrary client headers.




Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


Converting a Debian or Ubuntu Package Installation to Source


Use this procedure only on a supported operating system <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Requirements.md#supported-operating-systems>.
Changing installation ownership can affect paths, service accounts, package
upgrades, and database management. Test the migration on an isolated copy
before changing a production server.


Record the installed layout


Record the Cacti version, database and grants, PHP configuration, web-server
configuration, scheduler, service accounts, plugins, Spine configuration,
and actual RRD, log, cache, script, and query-resource paths. Distribution
packages can keep configuration and data outside the web application tree.
Inspect symlinks and package-managed files; do not assume that copying the
web directory includes their external targets.


Pause writers and take a complete backup


Follow Upgrading Cacti <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Upgrading-Cacti.md#stop-writers-before-backing-up>
to pause only Cacti's scheduler and other application writers, and take a
matching backup of the database, files, RRD history, configuration, and
external state. Do not stop the entire system cron service. Verify the backup
and rehearse restoring it before changing the package installation.


Stage and verify the source installation


Use the source installation guide <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Installing-Under-Ubuntu-Debian.md> to select and stage the intended
Cacti revision and dependencies in a new directory. Its fresh-install schema
import is for an empty database. Preserve the existing database for this
migration; do not import cacti.sql over it or run the fresh-install procedure
against production data.


Copy the existing configuration, custom resources, plugins, and RRD data to
the intended paths, preserving permissions. Reconcile the new configuration
example, URL path, web restrictions, dependency versions, and service accounts.
Protect credentials and secret files. Use the package maintainer's documented
procedure to relinquish package ownership without deleting application data.
Do not purge the package or accept a database-removal prompt merely to switch
to source. Keep the old installation and verified backup available.


Cut over and validate


With writers still paused, update the web mapping and the single Cacti
scheduler to the source installation. If changing Cacti versions, follow the
database-upgrade procedure <https://github.com/Cacti/documentation/blob/67bc24e44000/Upgrading-Cacti.md#run-and-verify-the-upgrade>
against the preserved database. Verify paths, permissions, plugins, collector
connectivity, and any remote-collector upgrades before resuming collection.


Check scheduled polling, logs, RRD updates, and representative graphs.
Document how future source updates will be applied and prevent a later package
operation from overwriting the source deployment. If recovery is required,
restore the matching files, database, RRDs, and configuration as one set;
restoring application files alone does not undo a database migration.




Copyright (c) 2004-2026 The Cacti Group


ADR-0003: Provide a versioned plugin extension boundary


Status: Proposed


Context


Plugins need to extend Cacti without depending directly on legacy installer
state, globals, raw persistence, or unstable domain internals. Existing hooks
must remain compatible while the platform evolves.


Decision


Cacti will introduce a versioned plugin manifest and extension registry.
Plugins will declare identity, compatibility, dependencies, capabilities, and
their supported lifecycle contributions. They will receive typed application
contexts and least-privilege service interfaces.


Installer extensions will contribute pure plan entries and worker-owned,
idempotent operations. Plugin operations are checkpointed by run, plugin,
version, and operation key. Required plugin failures prevent core terminal
success; optional failures follow an explicit policy.


Consequences


* Plugin authors have a stable compatibility contract and conformance tests.

* Legacy hooks are translated through a compatibility adapter during a
published deprecation period.

* Plugins cannot mutate install-run state directly.

* Core can report plugin lifecycle failures with plugin and run identifiers
while redacting secrets.


ADR-0004: Persist workflow state separately from diagnostic artifacts


Status: Proposed


Context


Installer state, progress, and diagnostics are currently spread across global
settings and logs. This makes recovery, support, retention, and secret
redaction difficult to reason about.


Decision


Relational storage is the source of truth for install runs, operation
checkpoints, leases, and structured events. Each record is scoped to a run ID
and uses atomic state and lease updates. Verbose logs, support bundles, and
large generated artifacts are stored separately with metadata and checksums in
the relational store.


Run options, checkpoints, and events are schema-versioned and validated.
Secrets are not copied into run records, events, logs, or artifacts; protected
configuration stores secret values and workflow records retain only references
or redacted values.


Consequences


* Operators can diagnose a run without reconstructing state from global logs.

* Retention can prune successful details while preserving failed-run evidence.

* Plugin operation data can use generic namespaced checkpoints instead of
creating arbitrary installer-state tables.

* Storage migrations, indexing, artifact cleanup, and retention policy are
required parts of the implementation.
